WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Duke and Duchess of Argyll.—Princess feraise Duchess of Argyll has been much in Society daring the London season, and will spend the autumn as usual, in Scotland. The Duke and Duchess will stay most of the time at Boseneath. The new tenaat of Inverary Castle. is a Mr. Jordan, of Boston, America. A good deal of interest is being, taken on the Argyll estates just now, over the renewed attempts to recover the Spanish Armada treasure, which is supposed to be at the bottom of Tobermory Bay, off the coast of Mull. During the past two or three hundred years, the Duke of Argyll's family has made occasional attempts to recover the treasure, and there are, at Invertry Castle several pieces of cannon and relics of aucient weapons which have been brought up from the wreck. There is now however, a modern salvage plant at work, and great interest is being taken in the operations. These are not being conducted by the Duke' but he receives the half of all that may be recovered. Will Motoring cease to be fashionable.—Motoring says" The World "is already on the downward grade, and according to that well known journal, it will have ceased in a couple of years time to have any great attractions for the fashionable classes. As they took up the bicycle and dropped it, se it is thcrught they will discard the motor car, and, it is also suggested, find some other way of showing their frieeds that money is norobject. That seems rather a spiteful remark from a journal which, if anything, is representative of the fashionable world. It is however evidently mot best pleased with motorists and motor cars, which, it sug- gests, will before long be employed only by trades- people, omnibus companies, and business purposes generally. Ducal Motbrists.—The Duke and Duchess of West- minster have been doing a lot of travelling during the summer by means of their motor car. They at least have no occasion for shewing their friends that money is no object, and therefore such travelling must have other attractions for them, as both the Duke and Duchess are enthusiastic motorists. During the summer they have made several journeyings ,by motor car, between Eaton Hall and London and Madresfield Court near Worcester—the seat of this Earl and Countess Beauchamp. For such journeys as from London to Chichester, for Goodwood, where the Duke and Duchess will be present for the racing, the motor car is a more convenient mode of travelling than the railetay, and it is not unlikely that they will use the same means of proceeding to Scotland, where the Duke andDuchoss will spend greater part of August and September. Ladies' Cticket.—Femenine cricket has made much more progress in Australia than in this country and there is talk in the Colonies of sending over next years ladies team of professional cricketers. In Sydney and Melbourne matches are played between the various ladies' clubs and colleges and it would probably be difficult to get together a ladies' team over 'here who would prove the equal of the best-trained Antipodean players. Perhaps one reason why femenine cricket has made so little progress is the inconvenience of women's dress, and it was this, so it is said, that led to the practice of overarm bowling. Up to the early Victorian days the old-fashioned underhand bowling was universal, but a school-girl who used to join in the game with her brothers found as she grew up that long , skirts and the crinoline greatly impeded her movements and she it was, so history relates, who first invented the round-arm system of bowling. The Cult of Self-ControL—The summer would seem to be the proper time to join the cult for self-control as there is no doubt that the hot 'weather makes many people melancholy, low-spirited, and irritable. The new cult is specially adapted to overcome these weak- nesses, as it prescribes that members must lead simple and natural lives in everything appertaining to is'iatary, . exercise, pleasures and amusements. That is a rule which must be beneficial to our mental and physical condition, but in case of a lapse, it is made a point of ' honour that a member must fine himself or herself for, • every cross word, irritating answer, or discourteous act that may be uttered orcornmitted. Members must also s pledge themselves to refrain, to the best of their . ability from the worrying habit, and they are advised ; to go about this in a methodical manner by dismissing from their minds such subjects as these is neither cause • for, or good to be done by werrying about them. There was a depth of meaning, if inaccuracy ef phrase, in the remark of the man who said' f have had many worries, most of which never occurred," and if he cult wordd help as to overcome this weakness, there are few of us who could not.become members without advantage to ourselves and our friends. Elbow Frills.—Therrp-to-date elbow sleeee is finished with a plain band of lace, dangling frills and floating' flouncing being numbered among the fashions past and gone. Possessors of very thin arms, will lament this decree, but if wise they will insist upon retaining some- thing in the way of softening draperies, to hide-the angles. It is preferable always to study What really suits one, rather than whet is fashionable. "Undoubtedly the banishment of OUT pretty dibow frills is due to' the craze for Bridge. The frills had an unhappy knack of sweeping the cards about to a distracting degree. The Bead-Work Bag.--Those women possessed of an old-fashioned bead-work bag, should take it out and about, such a possession being considered a most desir- able one at the moment. Of imitations thkte are plenty but taken as a whole, these are not suceessful. The new beads seem to lack the delicacy and transparency of the old-world varieties further, the favourite glaring gilt clasp is a most undesirable subetitute forthe dainty cut steel, or enamel specimens, beloved by our ancestresses. _ _ . .

Velvet ribbon loops and ends.—Long black velvet ribbon ends, falling from a cluster of velvet loops, are being attached to many of the newest hats and toques. Usually these fall at the back. though some milliners place thew slightly on the left side. The mode is a pretty one when the best of material is in question, but it will auickiy become all to popular, and should there- fore be indulged in while it may. The Washing of Pale Blue.—Pale blue is the leading color this summer, and as many of us have discovered to our own coct, it often fades dreadfully in the washing process. We shall have little to complain of however, if the articles are cleansed in the following instmer. Make a lather of yellow soap in warm water, in which the garment should be rubbed until cleamit should next be nosed well hi clear warm water, then plunged isito boiling water in which a piece of alum—the size of a walnut to every gallon—has been dissolved. This gradually deepens the shade of blue, and tho article should be removed when it shev.'s the right amount of

PENKETH.

MEETING OF THE PARISH COUNCIL.

A meeting of the Penketb. Parish Council was held in the schools on Monday evening when there were llresent Dr. Murray (in the chair), Messrs. W. Tiodgkinson, J. Ditch&id, Ashcroft, and E. Foster (clerk).

TIIE QUESTION er.THE MORTUARY.

At The last meeting of the Council the Clerk was instructed to write to the Cuerdley Parish Clerk and the Clerk to the bankey Parish Council suggesting a combination of the three par.:shes for the purpose of providing a mortuary. The Clerk said he had received a reply from the Cuersiley Parish Clerk stating that the matter would be brought before the next Parish meeting. Mr. J. Smith, clerk to the Sankey Parish Council stated that his Council could not see their way to joining in the scheme as they had entered into arrangements for the accommodation of dead bodies.

Dr. Murray said he mentioned -Coe matter at the last meeting of the District Council and wss informed that -the gu.stion was one for the overseers. The overseers had made the necessary arrangements.

TUE RATES.

Mr. Withinshaw, who was unable to be present, asked the clerk in a letter if he could give the amount of the education rate for Penketh. On the county call for sates 6d. in the 2 was put down for education and he thought the amount of the education rate for Penketh ,abould be indicated.

The Clerk said it was impossible to give the amount .of the education rate for Penketh.

Mr. Hodgkinson asked what Mr. Witlitenshaw's object in asking for the is:formation. Dr. Murray presumed it was curiosity And also to lot 'people know what .they were paying for education.

"JUST HKIISELF."

"The art of trying to please is gradually disare pearing; a woman gains more notice nowadays try affecting an open display of ennui." If we accept ; this indictment as true, it by no means follows, declares "Miranda," that we are filled with dismay. After'all, ths type of woman who is always "trying : to please" is not an acquisition upon all occasions. She ranks with the "funny man" and the anecdotal 1 bore; she is terribly apparent; obviously over• ! straining herself, and never capable of understand. ing that there is a time for silence as well as for : chatter. At the same time, we are not going to accept the chronically "tired" woman as her sub- stitute. Ennui is a pose that few women assume without causing irritation, and, upon the whole, we 1 should possibly find that the type of woman or girl which leads the way is she who is "just herself."

GARDEN:PARTY' FROCK.

I Of fine white lawn, so soft and fine that it lOoks almost like silk, our garden-party frock is of a dainty simplicity that is most delightful. On the full crossover bodice the little turn-down collar of black-and-white embroidered muslin is edged with ' a kilted frill of clear, soft-finished book-muslin. The same muslin, edged with Valenciennes lace, forms the dainty frilled vest, and the double "volaut". above the turn-back embroidered cuff on

the short full sleeve. ' Bafads 'of 'plain white an embroidered muslin hold in the fulness of the closely gathered skirt. A trim touch of smartness is afforded by the wide white belt of supple kid. The white chip hat bears a great bunch of roses and forget-me-note, and is further trimmed by a blue gauze scarf. A suggestion of the same • blue is brought into the gown by the blue-and-whits buttons on the collar and cuffs, and by the wee studs in the tiny box-pleat of the vest.

PHYLLIS IN LIVERY.

It lies been said many times that the solution of the servant problem will have been found when the calling of an ordinary domestic is by some means or another raised to the dignity of a recognised and honoured career for women. Various means, of bringing this about have been suggested. That of going in for a regular training and being duly certificated like the trained nurse or the board school teacher would ,be all very well if we were not too much in need of servants to afford to keep the unquali- fied at bay till these properly trained young women were ready. But it seems to me, writes "Ambrosia" ,in the World, that dress has a great deal to do with the matter—dress and liberty. The latter point is no .new one. but I believe that smart and really becoming uniform ,would largely help to attract young women to ...service. I would even gc. sofar as to waive the cap, though in my opinion it is most becom- ing.. But, st .411 events, in a well-cut, tailor-made ,dress of dark blue, dark grey, with a fancy ,waistcoat, collars and cuffs like nurses', and aprons and caps—if these are really indispens- able—according to fancy, any reasonable young -woman might well feel of some importance. The .uniform has unquestionably been the web which ;has caught many nurses, and I feel sure if as .much care were shown over the attire of our .snaikli,os, our menservants, and every family in- sisted upon pretty uniform being worn by their -women servants, more girls would cuter upon domestic duties.

white blouse is, a really good brrestment, especially if it of a nice quality materials for when washed it is almost as good as new. The blouse shewn in the sketch is intended for white material such as mercerised lawn or spotted muslin. iheyokr, extending ,down the centre of the front to

DELICIOUS RASE!: E It! Cl 7 JELL.—Put the picked fruit in a preserving pan; set the pan over the fire, stirring it the whole time; when it boils, take it off and strain it through a hair sieve, taking care co let no seeds pass through. Put the jelly or liquor into a pan and allow it to boil a quarter of an hour before putting in the sugar: stir it the whole cline, then add 14oz. of sugar to every pound of jelly. Then let all boil for a quarter of an hour, itirring it constantly the whole time. .When it is cool, put it into pots and sift powdered sugar over it.

' CALSTONITE.

A new product of the electric furnace. Ina been introduced in France under the designa- tion calstonite. It is a double carbide of barium and calcium, produced by M. Cartier, an electro-metallurgist of Mancioux, which decom- poses, on contact with water, like calcium car- bide.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
ALCOHOL AND THE HEART.

Dr. W. E. Dixon, of Cambridge, finds that alcohol in small concentrated doses increases the rate of the pulse and contraction of the heart, constricts tho deeper vessels, and increases the blood pressure, especially when the heart's action is feeble; it is therefore of great service in fainting. If much diluted it has little effect on the heart, but acts as a food, replacing starch -in maintaining the heat of the body. la too large doses it depresses the heart.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
TO KEEP MOTOR DRIVERS' HANDS • • WARM.

A novel device, the object of which is to TO. move . the discomfort of steering an automobile in cold weather, owing to the hands becoming numbed by contact with the metal of the wheel, has been patented. The steering wheel is warmed by the . water after its passage through the water jackets of the engine. This is done by means of a flexible tube connected to a hollow spoke, from which the water flows round the , wheel, thence returning to the water tank. It is claimed that in half a minute the wheel is rendered thoroughly warm. This invention promises to be largely adopted in public iservice automobiles, where great inconvenience arises in wintry weather from this cause.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW A DOG'S MIND WORKS.

The ,Professor of Philosophy in the Man- chester University has given to an audience 'of teachers the results of his study of the dog mind. The tale of the dog's merits and de- merits—it was an Irish terrier—his likes and dis- likes, and the reason " why " of each were un- folded. How much does a dog reason? was the question all the experiments tended to answer. From observing his dog, Professor Alexander has arrived at a rather modest estimate of a dog's mental power. He said his dog had learnt "how things go, but not 'the go' of them," or, in other words, his actions were intelligent, but not rational. He knew mechanically what effects followed from what causes, but not why they followed. Similarly, his virtues were not moral, they were merely habits; he did not know the reason for prohibitions—his sense of guilt was only the presentiment of a whipping.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
NOVEL GLASS PLUMBING.

In the development of the Moore electric light, which tills glass tubes with an appearance of glowing white smoke, many experiments have been made, says Science Siftings, with various metallic and non-metallic vapours under the in- fluence of the electric current. The secret gas now used is non-metallic. The light completely fills the tubes, and some of these are more than 150 ft. long. From this new method of lighting has sprung the new art of glass plumbing. The room or building is piped with lf-in. glass tubing, put up in lengAls of about %ft., and hermetically sealed in pomtion, and for this work has been devised a special set of glass-blowers' instruments, including hand torches, glass-cut- ting tools, portable pressure-blowers, &c. Joints at first declared by experts to be impossible, and taking half-an-hour when first accomplished, can now be made in less than a minute.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTRIC WAVES AND LIQUIDS.

An Italian scientist, Professor V. Buscemi, has made a series of observations in order to deter- mine the transparency of liquids for electric waves. For such experiments it is necessary to have a very sensitive apparatus for detecting the waves and making the comparative measurements. The author used an apparatus which is based, like Professor Fleming's, upon the magnetisation of iron by the action of the electric waves, but here be uses a galvanometer instead of a telephone. To produce the waves he places the oscillator in an iron case provided with a copper cover. In order to have a complete metallic continuity of the case, the border of the cover has a flange form, and dips into a circular groove containing mercury. In one of the sides of the case is a 2in. opening, over which he can dispose a glass tank some 0-25 in. thick. The latter is fixed to the case by a lead cement, which is found to be entirely opaque to the electric waves. In this way none of the waves can leave the case without passing through the liquid contained in the tank. By this arrange- ment Professor Buscemi found that among the liquids which absorbed the electric waves the acids occupy the first place, as their absorbing power is greatest. Distilled water is much less transparent than air, and the latter is less so than vaseline oil. A solution of salt (1 per cent.) is opaque when in a thin layer, also sea water which contains some 3 per cent, of salt. Ether, benzine, and petroleum are more transparent than distilled water, but less so than air.

LUMINOUS PHENOMENA OF THE HUMAN SKIN. In the Deutsche Medisinisehe Wochenschrift Pro- fessor Sommer records some interesting observa- tions made by himself on a luminous effect pro- duced after rubbing the human akin on incan- descent lamps. While grasping a small electric lamp one night, the Professor happened to observe that on contact with his hand the bulb of the lamp would shew a luminosity comparable with a mist of light, illuminating certain parts of the glass as well as his fingers, even before the electric current was completed. This remarkable phenomenon could be produced several times by rubbing the electric bulb with the hand. It should, however, be mentioned that not all electric bulbs are suit- able for the experiment, and that those which have been used for some time and shew the well- known dark coating of carbon particles are specially liable to failure. When rubbing a new or nearly new lamp, containing no metallic conductors, strongly on the skin of the forehead or lower arm, and withdrawing the lamp suddenly from the skin. the bulb will shew the luminous phenomenon. When withdrawing the lamp and stopping it sud- denly, its outlines stand out distinctly illuminated, while in the mithile a bright spot is observed. U after rubbing the lamp on one part of the body (e.g., the lower arm) some other part, as the cheek, .be touched with it, the contact will even without any friction result in a luminosity lighting up part ..of the face. When breathing strongly on a lamp that has been rubbed over some part of the body, a distinct luminosity is produced. According to rrofessor Sommer, the phenomena in question would be partly of a physiological character, that is to say, belonging to the human or animal organism. On.continuing his researches, he detected the photo- graphic action of the luminosity, and, as part of the same phenomena can be obtained also by friction on other substances, they would seem to be partly duerto some general physical law.

EXPERIMENTS ON PLANTS.

Iprofessor of the Cornell Agricultural Col- lege, America, has experimented on plant life with acetylene gas as an adjunct to the sun, with striking results. Plants grew to twice the size of those left to the nursing. of the sun only, and vegetables attained dimensions double those which were unassisted in the matter of illumina- tion. The experiments, says an American paper. were carried on through three months in a hothouse. The house was divided into two parts by a curtain. This curtain was hung in such a way that each half of the house received the same illumination from the sun. The beds on each side were exactly alike, not °Ally in size, but in location and contents. If a lily was planted in a .certain part of a bed en one side, another of the same species and age was set out in the same part of the corresponding bed on s the other. 1.10 ide of the curtain an equipment consisting of twelve thirty-five-candle-power acetylene lamps were erected on one side of the curtain. Ordinary tin reflectors over each light threw their rays cdownwards upon the soil. 'The lights were run whenever it was dark, the length of time ranging from nine to fourteen hours, according to the period of daylight. The temperature, Auld amount of moisture wqre as nearly the same on each side of the curtain as it was possible to make them. The plants on the acetylene side in many instances matured twice as fast as those on the other side of the partition. Radishes, onions, and several other kinds of plants which develop their edible parts below the surface of the ground were found to have pursued their usual course, except that they grew twice as fast as those not placed under the influence of the acetylene light. Several plants were not permitted to drink in any of the sunlight. They were brought out of a dark room into the rays of the acetylene light after daylight had faded from the western sky. Before the eastern horizon lightened they were again removed to the dark apartment, to re- main in utter darkness until the day should end. The narcissus which was subjected to this form of treatment blossomed, the flowers rp_maitt- Mg on the plant more than a weak.

For on it depended the safety and freedom of one for whom he would have gladly laid it down, if it had been so required. He hesitated whether to reveal his pre- sence or lie hidden till the men hadidisap- peared and he could make some effort to break through the door, He had as yet for- mulated no clear plan of escape, even if he succeeded in gaining an entrance into the cavern. The presence of Lady Agnes would make matters doubly difficult, for she, at any rate, had to be saved, even if he lost his own life in the attempt. The question was decided for him: Lipp made some coarse jest about Lady Agnes, and every word came clear to the listener's ears. Jordison laughed, and replied in the same strain. In an instant Holme's blood boiled with fury, the yellow light became a mist of blood, and he dung all caution to the winds. Thrusting one bare arm and clenched fist- through the opening, he yelled out the single word, ' liar," and the sound of it echoed and reverberated through the cavern like the shriek of winds. Lipp and Jordison both sprang to their feet and turned towards him. Then seeing the bare muscular arm extended towards them, they both laughed and came over to the door.

" Aint 'e 'evingly," said Lipp, with a broad grin. "Not yet," replied Jordison, with a quiet sneer, "but he will be presently. At least, we hope so, if he has lived a virtuous life— good morning, Mr. Holme. I trust you have had a pleasant night ? _•. . .

Hole did 'not answer, but drew back his arm and glared through the tiny opening in the door. He read his fate in the two faces that grinned back at him, the one bestial and repulsive, the other keen, gaunt, and wild, with the light of madness glinting in the dark eyes. _ .

Jordison advanced, and struck him in the face with his fingers. "Get back, you dog," he cried savagely. Holme reeled back, smarting under the blow. But he had made up his mind to be calm and conciliatory for the sake of the life that was so dear to him.

"Give me food and water," he cried, "for pity's take." "Oh 1 that's your song, is it ? " Jordison replied. "It's a tune we shall have to get accustomed to, eh, Lipp? Bt we won't be too hard on him the first day, eh, Lipp? Is the dinner ready ? " The servant went back to the steaming pot, and fishing out a piece of meat, thrust it into his mouth.

I " It's good," he cried out. Lay the table over here," Jordison called back; "we don't want to seem unsociable, and tell Lady Agnes that breakfast is ready." Lipp brought forward a small square table, set it down Within a yard of the door, and proceeded to lay it carefully. Then be crossed the 'cavern and disappeared. In two minutes' time he returned, and Lady Agnes Chile walked by his side. Her face was very white, and the dark rings under her eyes spoke of long hours •of suffering and sleepless nights. As she came into view, Holme cast one fierce hungry look at her and withdrew his face 'from the opening. • Jordison and Lady Agnes took their seats .at the table, and Lipp brought forward the hot stew from the fire and placed portions of it on their plates. He did not sit down him- self, but took a large allowance of the food to a distant corner of the cavern.

• During the meal Holme crouched silently against the door. He listened to the clink of knives and forks, and the hot scent of the • meat was agony to the starving man. Jordi- .son endeavoured to lend point to the situation by praising tbe victuals and making eulogistic remarks about the wine. At the end of the meal he raised his glass to his lips. "A toast," he cried suddenly, "long life to Mr. Arthur Holme I"

Lady Agnes Cliffe flushed, but for all that she raised her own glass to her lips and breathed a silent prayer, and it was at that moment that she caught sight of Holme's face at the window. He had decided to reveal himself in case she might be able to release him from his prison. She gave a cry of horror and started to her feet. Jordison laughed merrily. "Why, there is Mr. Holme," he said, in a mocking voice. How well he looks, seeing that he has not had anything to eat and drink for two days. I wish we could have asked him to join us, but it is, I am afraid, impossi- ble. Long life to Mr. Arthur Holme 1" Then a tender and delicately nurtured girl did a strange thing. She leant over the table and struck Jordison a blow in the face with all her strength. Before he could recover from his surprise she had plunged her hand into tbe hot mass of meat and thrust a few fragments of it through the hole in the door. Then she seized the half empty bottle of wine, but Jordison caught her wrist and pulled her back on the table with a crash.

How pretty," he said, with a sneer. "1 am afraid, Lady Agnes, I must ask Lipp to conduct you back to your apartment. Mr. Holme is starving, and so, much meat is not good for a starving man." , • " You lie!" yelled Holme. "1 am as well fed as you, and I could not drink any more if I wanted to." Then he wrenched at the door and beat against the panels with his bare fists till the whole -woodwork shook and rattled and the cavern resounded with the noise of his blows. Lipp came from his corner and led Lady Agnes away without any ,ceremony. and Holme and Jordison were alone. The latter came close to the door, and, lighting a• cigar, smiled at the white face at the window. " Well, Mr. Holme, I think you are fast this time."

"Where's Lord Harry Quy ?" Holma asked abruptly. " You will join him presently," answered Jordison with a grim smile, and then Holm* knew that Lord Harry Quy was dead 4

HAPTER XXVI/I JORDISON LEARNS THE TRUTH AT LAST.

ALL through that day Holme crouched against the massive door and waited to lay hold of such opportunities as chance might he pleased to throw to him. But hour after hour passed, and not a single ray of light broke in 'upon his mental darlmess. His fingers went over the door a hundred times until the position of every nail and screw and clamp was photographed clearly on his mind, He measured the thickness of the wood at the opening, and found that the door was made of two and a half inches of solid timber. He tried to ascertain how it was secured, but all the bolts and fastenings were on the other side. He only gathered one thing, however, front his investigations, namely, that it would be impossible to break it open without tools of some sort. A file, a chisel, a knife, even a steel nail, or a bit of broken pottery would have -given him a minute chance of successd -Hours of patient and undisturbed work with an instrument of this sort would perhaps have enabled him to loosen the hinges in the wall, or cut through the planks by the bolts, But he had nothing save his bare fingers and the task was hopeless. He saw nothing more of Lady Agnes Cliffei She represented his only hope of escape, and t was quite 'left:, in not the two men would not allow her the sThallest opportunity ox giving him any assistance.' She was doubt- less 'Securely confined in some other part of the cavern. The thought of her in some measure turned Holme's mind from his own personal suffering, but only by the painful process of substituting mental for physical agony. CHAP Jik XXVII. JORDISON'S RETREAT. t

Hours wrapped the thick sfieet twice round him so that the edge of it hung clear, from the ground, and rent two holes in it through which he passed his bare arms. It was a ridiculous and insufficient covering, but it was warm and grateful to a man whose shivering body had been exposed for hours to the cold air and water. Its chief failing as a garment was its insecurity. Tbis could have been remediedly half a dozen safety pins, but as these were not forthcoming, the wearer ,had to hold the folds round .his body with one hand.

Thus attired, -in -a costume whieh would have been ludicrous under more pleasing cir- cumstances, Holme set out to explore the tunnel in the other direction, and see ,vihethei the opposite end was as completely guarded as that which ran into the sea. He had but small hopes for the result of his investigation. Jordison's plans for .his death were -quite clear to him, and the knowledge was accen- tuated by the sorry jest of the coffin and the winding sheet. He was evidently destined to perish slowly of thirst and cold and starva- tion, in a tunnel of rock, securely sealed at either end.

But the young man's spirits, which had been almost extinguished by the cold water and the final barrier that had crossed his road to freedom, were rekindled by the compara- tive warmth of his covering. The first shock of the coffin and the shroud had been horrible, dealt, as it had been, in the semi-darkness and loneliness of the tunnel; and it had certainly been conceived and carried out with the sole 'object of frightening the unfortunate prisoner. But Jordison had underestimated the strength of Holme's mind, and was ignorant of its keen sense of humour, a sense that could extract a certain grim amusement• from the most dismal Circumstances. •

It was undoiibtedly this sense of the ridicu- lous that saved the young man's mind from breaking down at this Critical moment. Where others might have only seen the horror of the winding sheet, he could not help won- • dering how he looked in his strange garments, and the possible grotesqueness of his appear- ance appealed to his risible faculties. As to the coffin, he was already turning over in his mind how.he.could make .use of it to his own advantage. The real horror ,cif the situation lay in the pangs of thirst and hunger, which were no* furiously attacking his exhausted frame. There were at present/ no means of combating there ferocious adversaries, and no strength of mind 'could meet them with any chance of success. Holme could fight with fear, but not with his own physical necessities. However, he tried to thrust aside all thought of his sufferings, and. moved swiftly away from the sea keeping one hand on the rocky wall beside him. As he proceeded into the reces- ses of the tunnel, the twilight died away into darkness, and a last faint patch of light, seen far off like the gleam of a star, was finally hidden round . a bend in the passage. He counted his steps carefully as he walked, and tried to make every pace a y,ard in length. He could not afford to neglect the smallest and 'least likely source of information. When he had traversed about three miles, as near as he could judge, his bare foot struck some- thing hard and .there was a clink on the stone floor.

He stooped down and groped about in the darkness till his hand encountered a small jug Be had .overturned it, and the rock was wet with some liquid: He dabbed his finger of the damp stone and raised it to his mouth. Then he flung himself down and licked every inch.of the rock: The jug had been filled with fresh water. A :spoonful of it had collected in a small .crevice, and he tore his tongue in his. efforts to extract it. This was doubtless another of Jordiseh's pleasantries. A hundred yards further on he stepped on some round objects, that. gave and cracked beneath his foot. He stooped down again and thrust his fingers .into a sticky mass, full of sharp thin flakes. Be tasted it, and smacked his lips. Be had -crushed two or three eggs into a pulp. Feeling carefully „around him in the darkness, he came across a whole one. He broke off the top and swallowed ths con- tents. It was -a strong-flavoured egg—pro- bablv that of a mild duck, and not too fresh— but it seemed -very good to the starving man. When he had drained it to the last drop, and sucked the broken pieces of- the shell, he scooped up all he could get from the floor and enjoyed even that. it was no time for nice ceding. Of a :truth this ,Jordison was a merry fellow and full of devices. This scanty meal and the few drops of water he had managed to gather from the rocky floor put fresh life intco his body, and he moved forward more rapidly_ But it seemed as if the tunnel would never .cease,..and it was not till he had walked steadily for another hour and a half that he saw the •first sign of some termination to his journey.. All at once the veil of .darkness .was pierced by,. pin-hole .of light, a mere speck, se minute that Holine at brst thought it merely .the creation of his own brain.--one of those points of light that cross a man's eye in the darkness. But this one did not move, and the steadiness of it proclaimed its reality. He did not dare hope that it was daylight. But at least the glimmer of it ofte.racl some relielirom the dark- ness. pae lamp p small seen As he walked on, it grew larger and larger, squareandt of nf yellow;bcn through a tiny wind Ow. Then there came to sohfa his ears the distant murmur of voices, and then to his nostrils the glorloos smell of hot meat, wafted towards him like a breath of life, became sweet, luscious "'and alluring , beyond any temptation devised by Satan in this world. • He crept nearer and ncarer to the light, moving cautiously so as not to attract the at- tention of the speakers. The conversation grew more and more distinct, and soon he recognised Jordison's voice. At last he 'came to the end of his journey and his fingers traced out a massive doorway of wood, barred and clamped with iron. Ten feet to his right, and on a level with his chin there was a hole about nine inches square, and a long shaft of yellow Light streamed out from it into the darkness of the tunnel.

Holme sidled quietly along the door till he reached the aperture. Then he peered cautiously round the edge of it, well aware that he was bound to be seen if anyone inside hap- pened to be looking in that direction. But, as luck would have it, the only twc occupants of the vast cavern that opened out before his astonished eyes, were sitting with their backs to the door. The place that William Jorclison had chosen- for his sub- ierranean house was indeed princely in its proportions. It was lit with a dozen oil lamps, yet these did no more than make the darkness visible. The roof was hidden in a cloud of impenetfable gloom. Deep shadows floated ou distant parts of the floor. The voices of the two men reverberated from wall to wall of the apartment, and gave some idea of its ervirmons, size. The furniture that jordison had brought down with him from his residence above ground looked as though it .had been taken from a doll's house. The carpets, eighteen and twenty feet square, spread here ancl There over the floor, looked like little hearthrw,s.. The two men sitting before a large fire of crackling logs were no bigger than the forest pigmies of Central Africa. And over everytlying hung a gauzy veil of smoke that magnified heights and distances and lent an air of mystery-to the most corn- monplacc object's. Over the fire hung a great steaming kettle, and to Holme's sensitive nostrils the whole ' lace smelt like a kitchen. He saw at a glance that the two men were Lipp and jot.. dison. Scraps of their conversation came tc his ears, and only the echo of the cavern pre. vented him fr4l. bearing every word they u t f " 'E'd ought to be fat this time—l reckon he's cold—may his soul and body rot—that corfin, it makes me laugh—we fair busted their old machine—He'll speak now, I 'guess— Eh? dead ?—Not much. , The two men were in the cavern most of the day, but neither of them came near him, and they removed their table to the fire side, where they conversed in whispers, Holme watched them feed with hungry eyes, and the smell of their food nearly drove the starving man to madness. They paid no attention to him, as he peered through the little hole, They seemed to have forgotten his existence, The young man fully expected a further cross- examination by Jordison, and a promise of release in return for the information Jordison desired. He had already made up his mind to give way on this, if his submission would ensure the safety of the woman he loved. But Jordison did not give him the chance, and as he had decided that his confession should only be a last resource, he held his peace. Shortly after mid-day, as far as he could judge in a place where it was always night, he heard Lipp and Jordison busily tinkering with something against the wall in which the door was set, but he could not see what they were doing. An hour later a great black body slid Dut into the centre of the hall, and for the first time Holme had a clear and uninterrupted view of the famous black car. Something had been wrong with it on the night of the chase, and it was evident that Jordison was putting it in thorough order. He drove it several times round the cavern, steering with wonder- ful skill and accuracy. Then he came to :a stop and discussed its merits with Lipp. What struck Holthe most forcibly about the busi- ness was the utter contempt with which they treated him. They were not afraid to reveal their secrets to him. They talked loudly about the car, and Jordison laid bare almost all the secrets of its construction. He further disclosed how they had made use of the passage leading to the Great East Ray Quick-sands, spoke of how they had spent two months in making a. hard-way over them to the shore, where a small lane ran down to the edge of the sands, told how they had filled up the quaking surface of , the ground with rock taken from the mouth of the tunnel, and how this hard-way, covered by the tide with sand, had escaped the notice of the searchers. As they conversed, Holme realised that they were talking for his benefit. It was quite clear that they did not wish to conceal anything from him, equally clear that he did not count, that he was already regarded as a dead man. When they had finished their conversation the two men crossed the cavern and idisap- peared. Towards evening _Jordison came to the door and threw two small crusts of bread through the aperture. Holme gnawed them like a wild beast, though he knew that they were intended only to prolong the agony of starva- tion.

An hour later all the lights were extinguished in the cavern, and only the red embers of the fire glowed in the darkness. At last these died out, and Holme realised how much the light had meant to him. •He even missed the presence of his enemies, the sound of a human voice, and the sight of a human form. Any- thing was better than the silence and the loneliness, and the gloom of this subterranean world. Thoroughly exhausted in body and mind, he slept for a few hours, and awoke once more in darkness. He had been dreaming of the sunlignt and the sea, and the white foam before the bows of the "Rover." The black- ness suffocated him and he longed for a breath of fresh air and a glimpse of daylight. He rose to ,his feet, and made his way once more down the tunnel till he reached the water's edge. The tide was still between the bars of the gate. There he waited till the day broke, and the sun streamed in upon him. He, walked back into the tunnel and began to break the coffin into pieces. .He tore off the boards c'e by one and brok.. each of them in half uncle' his foot. Then, searching in the sand, he found an oyster shell, and with this rude pen he scratched a message on each piece of. wood. " Come to Triplets at once. danger.—Arthur Holme." Then he cast them, as far as he could, into the water, and prayed that at least one of them might float ashore into human hands. This done, he returned slowly through all the weary length of the tunnel, and crouched once more against the door which separated him from the cavern. Another day passed and still he caught no glimpse of Lady Agnes Cliffe, and still neither of the men paid any attention to him. Once or twice he called out to them, but they did not even turn .their heads. Towards nightfall Jordison again passed two small crusts of bread through the window. Holme found that a small tin half fall of water had been placed inside the door. That night he again went down to the other 3nd, and all through the day, so long as the tide was out, he sat close to the iron bars and tocked across the sea, waiting in vain for sonie boat to appear on the lonely waters. When he returned to the cavern end he found his clothes and boots lying in a heap against the door. He put them on (and felt more of a man. He also found the daily 'crust; of bread and another wineglassMl of water in a tin. And so he dragged out a terrible existence for four days' and then all at once his senses left hits, and he collapsed in a crumpled heap on the rocky floor.

The dying embers of the fire sank to a black' heap of charred wood and ashes, marked with streaks and splashes of dull red. " The cavern closed in upon the motionless figure i and the faint glow scarcely showed his face in relief against the encroaching wall of blackness. The silence was complete and unbroken, save for the ghogtly little noises that came now and then,from the glowing wood, Then suddenly there came a piercing cry of terror, and the rocky wall echoed it and tossed' it from side to side till it swelled into a long shriek of pain. Jordison started to his feet and stared round at the darkness with wide open eyes. For a moznent a cold fear gripped his heart. Then he laughed, and striking a: match, lit one of the oil lamps. The light disclosed nothing, but as he listened, he heard a faint mumbling and muttering in the distance. He smiled grimly. He realised that his victim was delirious, Anxious to gain some information from his ravings., he crossed over to the great wooden door and stood close to the opening. " West, still west," muttered the voice. " Haul in that main sheet a bit, the wind's shifting. What in God's name is all that whiskey for. Australia, eh? No, I won't consent. I will take her back to Valparaiso 4 A revolver, eh ? Well, it's a good argument." Then there was silence, and then low muttered sentences that Jordison could not catch, though he strained every nerve to grasp their meaning. Then suddenly he heard a scutte, and an arm shot out through the opening, and thin white fingers clutched the air. He seized the wrist and thrust it back, and he heard the heavy fall of a, body on the rock.

Then there was again silence, and then the voice began again to speak. "Two hundred thousand pounds," it cried out. "You scoundrel I A fair chance for life, eh ?—you devil, you missed that time: Ah, we're on equal terms now. A truce, eh? No! Well, take that and that, and that. My God, I've killed him I For Heaven's sake speak, Arthur! Speak, Sterious I" and the voice once more died away in an inaudible whisper.

Jordison leant against the door, and a cold sweat broke out on his body. The dying man had called out to Arthur Sterious, and yet—. " Dicky's tired," the vo,ce went on, "and where's mother, gone away on a visit? Wbat do these two ugly men wantol What do the fowls say, papa? Cluck, cluck, cluck, and cock-a-doodle-do. Ugh! what a splash the body made. Good-bye, Sterious, you brought it on yourself, you know. Cluck, cluck, cluck, and quack, quack. Oh, my God water, water ! " and the voice rose in a shriek of agony. But before it had died away, Jordison was tearing back the bolts and fumbling with the lock. Then he flung the massive door back with a crash, and fetching the lamp, leant over the form of the dying man and peered closely into his face.

A few moments later he had borne him into the cavern, and was pouring brandy and water down his throat, and Lipp, hastily summoned from his sleep, was rekindling the fire and pieparing some soup. For Jordison had at last come to the full knowledge of the awful truth, and knew that this was Richard Behag, and that he had bees torturing his own son to death,

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Fortnighty Meeting. The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present—Messrs.T. Baxter (presiding),C.Roylance, J. Bolshaw, H. Eyes, A. H. RELIEF TO NON-RESIDENT POOR. The Clerk said the Guardians of the Guiseborough Union had forwarded the following resolution which they asked the Board to support "That the Guardians present a memorial to the Local Government Board asking the Board to take steps to make it absolutely imperative on all Boards to pay relief to non-resident and non-settled poor when requested by the Guardians of other unions to do so. It was decided to support tle memorial of the Gains- borough Union. PAUPER LUNATICS. And there in the red glow of the logs he saw Mr. Woodward : Is thereany reason why they should the record of his own life. First, the bright not go for a holiday. The Master explained that they had been granteli flames of youth and ambition, then the roar- leave of absence but left before the date specified. ing furnace of love, and shame, and degrada- The minutes of the committee were approved. tion, then the steady heat of undying revenge, lit with a white hot flame, and now the flicker- ing of fires that had been nearly burnt out, and the red hot ashes that would soon be cold and grey. The whole history of his life passed before his eyes, and in the darkness of his subter- ranean refuge he could not find any distrac- tion from the memories of the past. During the last few months the danger and excitement of existence had allowed him but little time to think. His mind, unhinged by suffering and resentment of the wrongs he had endured; had found relief in wild excess and the pursuit of an inexorable revenge. His revenge was nearly complete. He had found Marie de la Mothe, and he had found Arthur Sterious, the man who had killed his son. The latter was suffering the agonies of a living death, within a few yards of him. The former was always within his reach dragging out a horrible exist- ence, with the fear of him ever at her heart, only waiting for him to strike the final blow. Lord Overcliffe was dead, and even Lady Agnes Clitie had not escaped him. He had resolved that she, too, should die, and that Lord Heatherstone should be the last victim. His scheme of revenge had included both the innocent and the guilty. He had decided that the whole of the cursed house that had allied themselves to Marie de la Mothe should share in her destruction,

Two lives in

-. Yet on this night, as he watched the red hot wood and listened to the tiny cracks and clicks that came from it, his mind seemed to have sunk into a deep gloom, and the fury that had given strength of purpose to his life had ebbed, and left his thoughts on a darkened shore haunted with gnosts of bye-gone years. His struggles as an ambitious young bank clerk, the days of his courtship and his marriage, the prattling voice of his son, the temptation and tile downfall, his dead wife, the hell nf convict prison, tile busy days in France, when he reconstructed a fortune from nothing, the search for the woman who had wronged him, and the son whom he loved, the loss of his money, the resolve to obtain more at any cost, the first robbery, ending in murder, the news of his son's death, a felon slain by a fellow criminal, the discovery of the man who had killed him, the discovery of Marie de la Mothe —the whole long record rolled out year after year before him, the various figures flittered past and mocked him, the voices of the dead whispered in his ears. He was presented With a complete biography of his life, written, not in words, but in scenes and pictures,. scarcely 'less real than the incidents themselves.

WARRINGTON IMPRESSED.

There's something frank and sincere about the word of a neighbour that never fails to impress Warrington people. Such honest proof is given week after week in our local press. What could be more convincing ? Mr. W. Williams lives et 20, Willis.street, Warring- ton. We have to thank him for the following letter "Dear Sirs,—?or some years I was a sufferer from pains in my back and abdomen, owing to my kidneys being out of order. The pins in my back were so bad at times that I could scarcely walk, and whenl sat down I was afraid to rise. I tried a great number of different medicines, but all to no purpose—they gave me no real benefit. At length I heard of Doan's Backache Kidney Pills, and decided to give them a trial. I procured a box, and can honestly say that I felt great relief after taking the first few doses. I continued with the medi- cine, and now I feel like a different man. I shall always heartily recommend Doan's Backache Kidney Pills to other sufferars with whom I come in contact. They are truly a wonderful medicioe.—Yours truly (signed) W. WILLIAIVS." Doan's Backache Kidney Pills are two shillings and ninepence per box (six boxes for thirteen shillings and ninepence). Of all chemists and stores, or post free on receipt of price, direct from Poster- McClellan Co., 8, Wells-street, Oxford-street, London, W. Be sure you get the same kind of pills as Mr. Williams had.

Trim or JrGs.

At Warrington, on—ft;e-sda-y,- Roukin, of no settled abode, was charged with stealing four small jugs from the Red Lion Vaults, on July 21, the property of John Lee. It was stated that the prisoner was in the vaults on Friday night, and later in the evening was found in Buttermarket-street, bleeding from the head having been knocked down by a man for using bad language. Sergeant Hodgkiss conveyed him to the infirmary, and the jugs were then found in his posses- sion. He was sent to gaol for three months.

TIIE FRE-;; CHURCHES.

..,

THE BAPTIST WORLD CONGRESS.

The chief event of the month among the Free ;Milts:hes has, of course, been the Baptist World Congress. Delegates from practically ever,/ country under the sun have been present in Liindoa in attendance at a series of meetings which can well be called a triumphant success. Pei haps one of the most notable lessons from this Congress has been the strength and power of tho Free Churches abroad. The Baptists In teroatiimally number far more members that, the Anglican Church in the world, told B,r,ptists are only one section of the Free 4...liiirehes. On iiiiday„ltily, 1(1, the principal pulpits. of the denomination were occupied by the visitors, and Baptias were 'able to eo joy fessis of oratory from their brethren from AmericA and other lands. The closing ii,ticunstLation at the Albert Hall will long be remembered. The presence of the venerable ProsiOent, Dr. l‘laclaren, was sufficient to give the occasion historic interest ; the scene at the ciose when the whole of the vast audience ebisped hands was a most memorable one. Ork,ati creoit is due to the Rev. J. H. Shakespeace, ALA., Sect etary of the Baptist Union, and to those who have helped to make the Congress such a success.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
A FREE CHURCH WELCOME.

The only deputation received by the Baptist World Congress was from the National Free Church Council, when the Rev. Thomas Law read tin address, calling to mind the great place the Baptists have occupied in the country's history. In an eloquent passage the address bays: "You can claim the greatest of alio- gorists and inspired dreamers in John Bunyan ; you have on your roll of writers John Foster, the Prince of Essayists, and Andrew Fuller, equally notable as a theologian, evangelist, and pioneer of foreign missions. You have had soul- winning preachers who have won the homage of Christendom, Robert Hall, C. H. Spurgeon, and Alexander Maclaren, who happily is still with us. Your missionaries have opened new worlds for the Redeemer, their names are familiar as household words—William Carey of India, Judson of Burma, Comber and Grenfell of the Congo, Timothy Richard and Afrhmore of China, some of whom are still earl 3 big on their work in a manner worthy of the great traditions they inherit. Indeed, you have been, and still are, rich in men whom the Nv ho le Church claims as its property." Dr. Horton also spoke, and Dr. Maclaren replied on behalf of the Congress.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE CHURCHMEN AND THE ELECTIONS.

It is interesting to note that the National Free Church Council has everything ready in its Electoral Department with a view to appeal to the country. Millions of leaflets have been print ed, and are all ready for distribution to the various centres. Arrangement have been made for speakers to travel throughout the country, and help to be rendered in other directions. Of course, the National Free Church Council is concerned with the election from the point of view of Education and Temperance, and is not touching other questions.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE CT:LURCH INtiTITUTEL

There is a movement going forward for a combined effort of a religious social nature ter combat the evils of the public-house. Under the auspices of Free Church Councils it is pro- posed to erect institutes in many parts of the country. The Scunthorpe Council, for instance, is making an attempt to provide for the needs of the young life of the town by the erection of inch an institute, which is to serve as a centre for the development of the spiritual, mental, and physical life of the young people of the distlict. The scheme propounds the raising of a building in a central part of the town, at • cost ot about f.,:rt{XY; of which £BOO would be for the site, and about £3OO for furniture. This example is also being followed in other centres. The Churches are more and more realising the necessity for social work of this character.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
ENGLISH AND SCOTCH FREE CHURCHMEN.

The Churches (Scotland) Bill which has been before the House of Commons does not only concern the Northern part of the kingdom. On the fifth clause, which introduces the question of more liberty to the Established Church of Scotland, two Free Church leaders, Mr. J. Compton Bickett, M.P., and Mr. R. W. Perks, M.P., protested in the name of the Free Churches against the introduction of this con- tentious element into the bill. The Organising Committee of the National Free Church Council passed a resolution strongly protest- ing against the clause.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
BURIAL RIGILTS.

The question of burials may be rather gloomy, but is undoubtedly causing some commotion ist the present time. For many years Free Churchmen have suffered various disabilities in the matter of cemeteries, burial grounds, &c., and these restrictions have only partly been done away with. An article showing the position of the case, and proposed future action, appears in this month's Free Church Chronicle, and should offer considerable assistance to those who have to deal with this subject.

SEASIDE CONFERENCES. August is supposed to be the slackest month of the year, but certtinly it seems as if this rule will not apply to the National Free Church Council, for three series of meetings are to be held during the mouth. Last year Seaside Conferences were held at Scarborough and Blackpool, and their success led the way to this being a permanent featur• in Free Church Council Work. The Conferences this year are to be held at Morecambe, Douglas (Isle of Man), and Blackpool. The first will be held at Morecambe on August 14. The series of meetings will commence in the afternoon with a sermon by Rev. F. B. Meyer, 8.A., and those taking part will include Rev. Thomas Law and the Rev. J. G. Stevenson: A Conference on similar lines will be held at Douglas (Isle of Man) on August 17 and 18, and still another on the 24th and 25th at Blackpool. At the last of these, however, the Rev. J. E. Rattenbury will take the place of Rev. J. G. Stevenson.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
CHELTENHAM CONVENTION.

It will he remembered that the Oxford Con- ference for Ministers inaugurated by the Rev. It. J. Campbell, LA., was at his suggestion taken over by the National Free Church Council. Unfortunately, owing to another series of meetings, the Oxford friends were not able to accommodate the Conference this year, and the centre has been changed to Cheltenham. The meetings will be held during the week commencing Sep- tember 18, and ‘vi!l follow the order of Dr. Horton's Conventions. There is to be a Social Reception on Monday. and on the following days the usual Fatly Morning Prayer Meetings. The Rev. R. J. Campbell. M.A., is to preach the Afternoon Sermon on Tuesday, and will speak on Wednesday morning. On Wednesday evening the speakers at the great -Free Church Demonstration will be the Rev. Thomas Law, Rev. F. B. Meyer, and Mr J. Compton Mellott. M.P. Mr. Meyer speaks at the Conference the following morning, and at the Young People's Demonstration on Thursday evening the Rev. Silas K. Hocking, Mr. R. W. Perks, M.P., and Mr. H. R. Mansfield, M.P., will speak. The Afternoon Conference will be devoted to the subjects "Free Church Council Work," introduced by the Rev. Thomas Law; "The Sunday School,' by Rev. F. B. Meyer B.A. ; and "Foreign Mission Work," introduced by Rev. James Pickett.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
A "PENNY WEIGHTER " DEFINED.

- - • - The Recorder (at the Old Bailey on Tuesday) : What is penny weighter ? Inspector Drew : A person who goes into a jeweller's shop, and with the aid of some sticky substance on the end of the finger picks up some small bit of jewellery. This is then attached to the under surface of the punter, and a confederate who follows appropriates it. The Recorder : tVe are always learning something.

LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE v. YORKSHIRE.

The Lancashire twenty-six had an uphill task before I them on Saturday when they met the Yorkshire team at Old Trafford. Distanced by the heavy number of 531 points at Airfield three weeks since, their team did not appear to have been sufficiently remodelled to altogether cover their opponents' majority in hand, but when half time was called the deficiency was covered, and by the time the game terminated they claimed a stronger majority than the visitors brought with them from Airfield. VOLUNTEER CHURCH PARADE.

The Rector's Criticism of Warrington.

The annual church parade of the members of the Ist Volunteer Battalion Prince of Wales's Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment) took place on Sunday morning, when that' attended the service at the Parish Church before leaving for the annual encampment on Salisbury Plain. A start was made from the Drill Hall at about ten o'clock, the total strength of officers and men being over .109. Colonel Edelsten was in command The procession was headed by the regimental and bugle band followed by the "mounted" infantry on foot, the rear being brought up by.the Cycle Corps. The men pre- sented a very smart and soldierly appearance. They marched at a quick pace through Legh-street, Sankey- street Buttermaraet-street, and Church-street to the church, the route being lined with spectators. The centre of the church was reserved for the men from the Dennt and the Volunteers, most of the usual congregation toeing accommodated with seats in the gallery. The service was conducted by the Rector, assisted by he Rev. T. A. J. Ridpath, and Mr. Darby was at the organ. There was special music and the choir gave an excellent rendering of the anthem "Judge me. 0 Lord, (Mozart). The Rector took for his text the word : Watch ye. Stand fast in the faith. Quit you like tmen, Be strong." He said these were rather short,sharp and stern words; words they might think better befitting the soldier on command than the minister of the Gospel of peace, and yet God had given to His ministers in things lawful and right authority and power. Theßector said he would first speak to the Volunteers as inhabitants of the town and borough as burgesses, as electors. They might in a sense compare Ephesus with Warrington, the latter being a free town of great importance. Its population'had trebled within the last 20 years, being now estimated at GB,OOO. It had a good government. He did not know of a town that was better ordered and better governed than Warrington. It had its nine wards and four officers appointed to each—an alderman and three councillors—whose duty was to take care of the ward and do their utmost for I it. They were positions of great importance, but to which everyone present might rise. There were I many trades in the town, and that was said to be the reason of its prosperity. Where ons . failed another succeeded. There were, however, dark blots, physical blots, moral blots ; smoke filled our atmosphere to a certain extent, perhaps unnecessarily. Sewage filled our rivers quite unnecessarily. There was drunkenness, gambling, impurity, and it WAS the bonnden duty of everyone of them to do his utmost for the good of the' town. In the second place the Rector spoke to the men as soldiers, as volunteers, as men holding a grand position in the Ist Voluntoer Battalion, South Lanca- shire Regiment. They had important duties to dis- charge. Let them think of the possessions this country I held at the present time. It was almost within our own remembrance that our country had gained these great possessions and as Volunteers and AS soldiers they must be determined to stand up for the position which they believed God had given them for the welfare of man- kind. And now the Warrington Volunteers were going into camp to prepare themselves for the duties which one day might come upon them. They had to be very careful about attention to their duties, obedience to command, and when off duty they must use pore language and indulge in innocent amusement, thus keep- ing up the honour of the country and their regiment. In the third place the congregation were addressed as , Christians. As townsmen they loved their town and as soldiers, they loved their coantry. So he would say As Christians love your church.'' The narch back to the Drill Hall was witnessed by a still larger number of !spectators. Church-street being rendered almost im- passable for the time being. The men marched quickly , through the streets, and the headquarters were reached shortly after twelve o'clock.

3EWSRY ST., WARRINGTON.

Mr. BAISOUR has PRECEDENT AND committed the fate PRACTICE. of his Government for the remnant of the Session to the partisans who gave him their pledge at the Foreign Office, and forgot it a few hours later. We shall see whether they are worthy of this second trust. The Prime Minister is warned by his most faithful organs in the Press that another defeat will be fatal. This is scarcely kind to the case he set out on Monday in regard to Constitutional practice. If that be accepted, there are no conceivable circumstances, as Mr. ASQUITH said, in which the Government need trouble themselves about ,hostile division. Mr. BALroon appar- ently arranged his precedents so as to guard him- self against any further lapses on the part of his adherents. If they should tail him again he has only to quote his favourite precedents once more. But this is evidently not the view of the Unionist journals. "The Globe,"for instance, is so far from being impressed by the Constitutional practice, as edited by the Prime Minister, that it dismisses with scorn.:"These snatches of history culled from he Parliamentary records of 70 years " as" some- what too much of the lamp." They belong to "a storehouse from which the most contradic- tory propositions can be disinterred." Mr. Balfour is likened to a lawyer, who can " find authority for every doctrine of equity." Let us have none of the "tyranny of precedent," says "The Globe." Let us look at the pt esent state of public affairs. We are quite willing to look at it, but "The Globe" discovers that reople who do not see the state of public affairs from its stand- point prefer "the squabbles of a faction to the interests of an Empire." When the constituencies proclaim with overwhelming unanimity at the bye-elections that they are sick of this Govern- ment, their opinion is dismissed as a faction squabble It is only a totally unrepresentative Ministry that is deemed capable of safeguarding the Imperial welfare?

If this view be accurate, it is obvious that Mr. BALFOUR would remain in office even after defeat at the General Election. Wnat is the use of telling the Ministerial members that they have the life of the Government in their hands for the next fortnight or three weeks, it the interests of the Empire are Mr. BALFOUR'S personal property? and cannot be taken from him by a "squabbling faction," however:victorious in the division lobby? The logical consistency of this is not quite clear, but the Unionist papers almost with one accord declare that the country must be saved in spite of itself. Everything would go wrong in our relations with Japan and France and Europe generally if Lord LANSDOWNE should be driven from the Foreign Office. But the country, if it had the chance, would not hesitate to commit those relations to the leader of the present Opposition. The country is entitled to make these changes, but Mr. BALFOUR persists in deny- ing it the opportunity. There is not the smallest reason to suppose that Japan would adopt e different attitude towards a new Foreign Secre- tary. She knows our constitutional history too well. She would expect the new Foreign Secre- tary to take up the negotiations where he found them, and she would not he disappointed. France would not be disappointed. An the solemn warning from Mr. BAIXOUR'S friends 'about the interests of the Empire is a hollow pretence. He said on Monday that it was only on his own supporters that he urged his claim to be considered the trustworthy guardian of those interests ; but his own supporters are urging it on the nation at large. They are drawing pictures of the awful things that must happen if the Foreign Office should pass under the control of Sir EDWARD GREY. Perhaps some reformer of the Constitution will tell us next that the country should content itself with leaving Mr. BALFOUR and his principal colleagues at their posts, and appointing a few Liberals to minor offices in the Administration. This would not be more absurd than the line of argument which is adopted by the Unionists to meet the present case.

So far as the action between the traders and the Ship Canal Company is concerned, the °or- pora.tion appear to have no locus stamii, and by the judgment of the House of Lords on the appeal they are struck out of the action. The traders have achieved a signal victory, for not only are Mr. Justice Byrne's findings re-established, but the Canal Company has to pay all the costs of the traders in this appeal and in that of the Cour!, below. But the position of the Corporation is much different, and the fact that they have bee," 11 non-suited is leading ratepayers to ask what their magnanimous support of the traders' case has cost the town. It appears to us that the Corporatioa

I

I

I

has, gone further than was required in the public Interest, ar.d some explanation, with a statement of the cost involved, would be welcomed at the Council meeting on Tuesday.

We are glad to note that the General Purposes Committee of the Corporation which comprises the whole Council, is not going to accept Admiral Nares' dictum that the proposed widening of Warrington Bridge will render the navigation of the river more difficult than at present. Last Weak we were informed that the Admiral had used the adjective "dangerous" in alluding to the effect of the alterations on navigation, but judg • jog from the minutes, his expression of opinion is not so strong as that. He may yet be amenable to reason and it is satisfactory to find that the Committee has resolved " That it be an instruction from this committee to the Bridges Sub-Committee to do their best to prevent the widening of the Warrington Bridge being put off,and that they ap- point a deputation to wait upon and obtain expert evidence to lay before Sir George Nares in favour of such widening." There is hope yet for the scheme which will combine efficiency with economy.

'Whether there is a general election in October or not—and the odds against are not excessive— the Liberals in Warrington will be found " ready, aye ready!" For some time they have been nursing and educating the constituency, system- ati zany and persistently, and as a party have Probably made a b4gger forward movement locally than at any time during the last 20 years. The Popularity of the Progressive cause to-day is remarkable, and only equalled by the national nausea at the unconstitutional and extravagant behaviour of Mr. Balfour and his Govern- Ment. Liberal meetings in Warrington are of Llmost weekly occurrence, and the addresses which have been delivered from time to time by Liberal Labour candidates have done much to cement the Progressive forces and bring recruits to the ranks. This week we have had a visit from Mr. F. Neilson, the candidate for Newport (Salop) Division in opposition to Colonel X.enyon Slaney, and he has well maintained the high standard of political oratory which preceding speakers like Messrs. H. Vivian and F. Maddison have established. The audiences have been most enthusiastic in their approval of Free Trade,Home Rule, Educational'and land,law reform, and other topics which are to-day in the forefront of advanced politics. There is now something like an educated public opinion in Warrington on national affairs, and we have the fullest confidence that that opinion will weigh heavily on the side of Mr. Crosfield when the time comes for him to raise the standard 'of "peace, retrenchment and reform."

Colonel C. E. de Is Poer Beresford tells the story of the rise and fall of the Russian Navy in the ' Fortnightly Review." The history is a peculiarly ,sad one. Russian maritime power has never been great in the sense that one speaks of British or French naval predominance, but the successive rulers of the great Empire have had many capable sailors who have striven to bring the navy up to the standard which they saw was requisite if it Were to be fit to contend for the mastery of the seas. That the navy is now practically annihilated is not their fault. It is due rather to the constitutional indisposition of the Russian for the sea. Nothing has been done to'rnake him like it. The sailors, recruited from the fields of , the interior, have not even been trained in , the profession for which they have been chosen. They have been land fighters trans- ferred to the sea against their inclinations. ' Colonel Beresford attributes the failure of : the Russian Navy very largely to this. The ineptitude with which the personnel was ; treated extended to the niaterigßussia's attempts - at shipbuilding in recent years have contributed , to her disasters. It is curious to recall the points .". of parallel between Russia and her conqueror in , the matter of the navy. Peter the Great, to whom the Russian Navy owes its foundation, learnt all be could about ships in Holland and England, and , applied his knowledge with advantage, as the F Japanese have gleaned knowledge from the West. ißut those who followed him did not keep up the ‘ nut they forgot that it is not enough to have ” nips—you must also have men. Japan did not + forget that point, and so she won.

pNOTES AND NOTIONS.

Motormen and conductors on the trams are to be , paid time and a quarter in future for Sunday ' labour. After much agitation and delay the men have ' thus obtained what appears to be only elementary ; justice. * * * Councillor Maginn possesses a good deal of natural wit, but on Wednesday evening he raised a laugh un- consciously by an excellent "bull." Speaking of the " distressful country" he said " Ireland is peopled by the ruins of homesteads and farms !" * * * A Runcorn Rural Councillor remarked on Tuesday , that before the well at Norley was cleaned (?) out, the ' Water was beautifully clear "like gin." Now it was not fit for pigs to drink. A sample produced at the , Meeting resembled beer more than gin, but was prob- ably neither, or there would be no complaints from * * * A As an incentive to regular attendance, good con- „..ucct, and general proficiency,Toller's Charity Trustees 411Mgborough (Lincs)., offer prizes in boots and cloaking to the children attending the council school. The result is said to be very satisfactory.lt ought to be. some children expect is a licking when they are absent ! * * * , The Victoria Park Bowling Green seems to be k Popular with the workiugmen, and during the recent 'une weather it could be seen any afternoon or ' 'Terttli• g well-filled with local trundlers. The receipts from the 27th June to the 19th July are as follows, )742 tickets at 2d. £l4 10s. 4d. ; 791 tickets at id. ~13 ss. 11d. * * * The mythical ‘• ashes " remain ! Australia's cjicketing giants have fallen from their h;gh estate and • l'een compelled to bow the knee to the Mother Coun- try. England's superiority has been strikingly Manifested and the conclusion is unavoidable that -Australia. has struck a lean time. Cricket form seems t to run in cycles, and just now it is the old country's 'tarn to reign supreme. * * Wednesday evening's downpour forced the Liberals to hold their meeting in Sharp screet mission, and the scovery was very soon made that there was no gas. 41r.
