* * * t, The Gymnasium Committee has evidently come to ~ 'te wise conclusion that" half a loaf is better than bread" and has informed the Education Committee -tlkat it is prepared to consider a scheme for the gYtnnastic instruction of the school children. A sub- jot:l.rnmittee is appointed with full power to make all .4±4l:lgements. By the way we notice that the instruc- :yn, is this time described as " gymnastic instead physical." Is this merely an accidental con- , 1,4210 n of terms? There is considerable difference 0 •-•tiveen the two kinds of instruction.

The I. Heathens " and the Corporation do not ?xactly see eye to eye and on the question of education Ilave in turn threatened to exclude school children ; e°n:ll from the other's district from attendance at s ools under thBir controL The villagers are aPParently to be the first to move, as, provided the 'abjection to the County Council's proposal to build nevi schools in the village is successful, they have Practically decided to decline to allow Warrington Children to attend the Stockton Heath Schools. If the ‘,orporation take it into their heads to retaliate the heathens " might find it a case of the biter bit. * * There have been two curious commentaries on the be Ttting evil this week, which illustrate what a differ- ence there is in the point of view. At the City Temple on Thursday the Rev. R. J. Campbell said "he would aPPeal to the highest in the land. The King was one of the best Sovereigns of the world. He had taken tTrae of them by surprise, and had earned the title of Ldward the Peacemaker. He well deserved it.

Would to God that by their prayers King Edward might be influenced to see the necessity of distinguish- ing between gambling and sport, and use his mighty influence to purify sport and distinguish what was good from what was evil. He spoke with all respect. Only the power of the King of Kings could rid them of the sin of hazard."

* * * Police activity in the suppression of Manchester street betting has raised a protest not against the evil but on its,behalf. The Manchester Centre of the National Sporting League has sent a. letter to the Watch Committee, pointing out that the business of the bookmaker is "a respectable and legitimate one." While deprecating the betting by women and children, the League contends that the working-class's of the country ought to have equal facilities with the leisured classes to bet if they desire to. But the most remarkable feature of the letter is the suggestion that the Corporation of Man- chester should grant facilities for betting by charging all bookmakers a registration fee and assigning to them "places such as waste ground, where they could assemble for two hours at noon and two hours in the evening, where working people may bet with free- dom." The idea may have something to recommend it, but we can hardly conceive a sane municipality, creating public betting grounds for the furtherance of the vice—for vice it is notwithstanding the legal sophistries of the National Sporting League. There has been a " chiel " among us taking notes. Mr. Bart Kennedy, a well-know globe-trotter, has tramped through Warrington and sailed through Widnes on the Ship Canal. Of both places he has something interesting to say, and the comparison is most flattering to Warrington which he des- cribes as " a picturesque, clear-aired, pleasant town." This is quite the best thing we have heard from outside of the ancient borough. It must surely be less black that it is occasionally painted by anti- smoke crusaders. But Widnes! Poor Widnes! Mr. Kennedy lets himself go here with a vengeance. "This poisonous hell-town," dark, dreadful, picture," Sm. Widnesians are writhing in their wrath, which is not lessened at all by Warrington's self-complacent and audible smile. A correspondent, defending the town of many smells makes significent allusions to a nice little cemetery. But probably Mr. Kennedy will not return via Widnes!

* * * The Warrington Football Club's fixture list has been made up, and readers will find a copy in another column. Several prominent clubs are missing from the list, notably Bradford and Hull Kingston Rovers, but there is a possibility of dates being booked with them later. It will be noted with surprise that a new Lancashire Cup Competition has been inaugurated,the first round to be played on October 14th, and the whole thing to be completed in December. Warrington are drawn to meet Rochdale Hornets away in the opening round. All the other fixtures are League fixtures and points will be reckoned for wins and draws, with a percentage after the style of the cricket championship. Then at the end of the season there will be the Cup Competition proper for the whole of the Union. The innovations which are to be tried during the ensuing season are certainly not popular, but we think that the supporters of the Warrington Club will see, as heretofore, keen and interesting football.

Munn THE CLOCK for Good BOOTS.—Advt. Warrington Workhouse Master Honoured.

At the annual meeting of the National Association of Workhouse masters and matrons, Mr. J. F. Awberv, master of the WarringtoaWorkhouse was unanimously elected president for the ensuing year. At the meet- ing of the Board of Guardians on Wednesday Mr. Forshaw remarked that this was the first time that honour had been conferred upon a Lancashire master. They in Warrington had learnt the value of Mr. Awbery's work and were pleased that he had been so honouredby the National Association. Mr. Billinge also congratulated Mr. Awbery and wished him a happy and successful year of office.

PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER TALE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Warrington Union and the Unemployed. At a meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday it was decided to engage men to paint the Workhouse. It was pointed out that this would be more economical than having the work done by contract and would also find work for the unemployed. It was also decided to engage men to paint the Padgate Schools.—A.t the same meeting of the Board, Mr. Thompson, Mrs. Clay, Mr. Awbery and Mr. Billinge were appointed a deputation to attend the annual conference of Poor Law Unions to be held at Lancaster in September. It was also resolved that Mr. Yorke's bill of £3 15s. for cleaning and repairing the recently purchased second-hand set of musical instru- ments for Padgate schools be passed for payment.

LOCAL NEWS.

The Examiner, Saturday, July 29, 1905.

!Warrington Cycling Club. Twelve members turned out foi the run to Davenhana on Saturday arrived at their destination, they put up lat the Bull's Head, bowling being indulged in after tea. On Tuesday a visit was made to the Raven Inn, Glazebury where 15 members who made the run also spent an enjoyable time on the bowling green. Music in the Park. The Borough Brass Band will play the following programme in the Park to-morrow (Sunday): —Hymn, , Stourbridge "; march, "Punchinello," W. Rimier; overture, "Poet and Peasant," Suppe ; selection, "Rossini," Round; trombone solo, "Ora Pro Nobis," Piccolomini ; soloist, Mr. James Mannion ; selection, " Stradella," Flotow ; "God save the King"; con- ductor, Mr. T. Griffin,

Cockhedge Works Picnic. The managers and overlookers of Messrs. Armitage and Rigby, Limited, Cockhedge Mills, held their annual picnic on Saturday last, when Belle Vue was the favoured place. The party travelled by the 12.55 train from Central Station and then by tram to the gardens. After making a tmr of inspection of the varied attractions of the gardens the party assembled in the dining rooms where an excellent spread was provided by Messrs. Jennison, to which the party did full justice. After tea thanks were voted to the firm for providing these annual picnics. The party then again adjourned to the gardens, where a very pleasant time was spent. Band of Hope TrIP. The members and friends of the Hill Cliffe and Stockton Heath Band of Hop, to the number of about 120, had their annual excursion on Thursday to Rhyl. The party left Warrington at 8.30, and arrived at Rhyl at 9.45, the weather being charmingly bright. Sea- side enjoyments of various kinds were indulged in by the younger members of the party, while others made excursions to Rhnddlan Castle, l3odelwydden Church and St. Asaph. A substantial :tea was provided at 3,30, at the Grosvenor Hotel, the catering of Mr. James giving every satisfaction. A start home was made at 8.30, and the ptrty was safe at Stockton Heath by 10 o'clock, after having spent a most enjoy- able day. Admirable arrangements for the outing had been made by a Committee, with Mr. G. Whitfield as hon. secretary. Councillor Pemberton and the Water Committee.

Councillor Pemberton will move at the Council meeting on Tuesday: That myself and four mea.bers of the Council shall have access to all contracts, books, papers and letters in reference to Lawson's contract, and this Committee shall have power to inquire into the very large amount spent on this contract over and above the contract price of £24,000, And report its findings to the Town Council.—On the Water Com- mittee's minutes is the following: An application from the Investigation Committee for production of the following documents was read :—(1), Specification of Mr. James Lawson's contract ; (2), Orders for 1901 ; (3), Letters written by Water Engineer to Mr. Lawson in 1904 ; (4), Lawson's Bill for 1904, charging for work done. Resolved: That all the documents with the exception of the specification of , Mr. James Lawson's contract be produced to the Investigation Committee at their next meeting. '

WATERS. —Advt. Supposed Hoax at Warrington. A bottle was on Tuesday recovered from the Mersey at Arpley. On being opened it contained the following message :—" Howley, July 24,1905.-1 William Twist has drowned myself,because my mother turned me out of home. I drowned myself at the Twelve Arches." Sergeant Barlow has made inquiries in the Howley district, but up to the present no one of that name has been discovered.

• "PREMIER" CYCLES at 12 guineas with change speed gear ttc., unectualled.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge st.,Warrington.—Advt. Death of Mrs. T. Woods. The funeral of the late Mrs. Woods, wife of Mr. T. Woods, Boot maker, Church street, took place at Hill Cliffe on Sunday last. Deceased who was in her 74th year had been confined to the house for the past 17 years and passed away on the 20th inst. The chief mourners were Messrs. T. Woods (husband), T. Woods and J. Woods, (sons), H. Allender, T. Hewitt, A. Andrews, E. Oxley, (sons-in-law), W. Woods, James Woods and J. Taylor (brothers-in-law) F. J. Whittle and many others. A large number of floral tributes were sent by relatives and friends.

Trinity College, London: Pianoforte playing. Junior Division, honour; Bessie Evelyn Whitlow. H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC takes away that tired feeling.—(Advt.) Improved Service to London, &c.

As will be seen by an advt on our front page the Midland Company announce new and improved ser- vices to and from Warrington in their July Time Tables. A train leaves Warrington at 9.55 a.m., pro- viding a new through service to Ilichniond,Folkestone IDover, Deal, flastings,Margate,Ramsgate, Portsmouth, Southampton, and other resorts on the South Coast, thereby avoiding the inconvenience and expense to Ipassengers in crossing London. Through carriages are run on the 10.30 a.m. train from Manchester (Central) to several stations on the South Coast. A similar return service is given by a train due to arrive Manchester (Central) at 6.10 p.m. A new ser- vice is also given to Sheffield, West of England, Mid- land Counties, and London by train leaving Warring- ton at 1.13 p.m. The service to the Eastern Countries for the summer has been greatly improved. PUBLIC BENEFIT. UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—AdVt. Poor Children's Summer Camp. The first contingent of girls for the camp at Prestatyn left Warrington on Saturday morning. By permission of Colonel Edelsten the children, to the munber of 110, assembled at the Drill Hall at 9.30, where they were met by Mr. Jenkins, the hon. secretary, and Mrs. Wilkinson and her staff of seven lady assistants. They were marshalled in processional order and headed by the Blue Coat School Band, started for Bank Quay station, which they left by the 10.23 train, all happy in the prospect of a good holiday. Prestatyn was reached in gond time, the train being met at the other end by Mr. A. H. Lea, to whom the chief credit is due for initiating this annual trip to the seaside. A great deal of rain fell on Saturday, but beautiful weather has prevailed for the greater part of the week Next Saturday another contingent of girls will take the places of those who will return home after a week in camp, and then the poor boys for the ensuing fort- night will have their turn. The Hon. Secretary acknowledges with thanks the receipt of potatoes, and hampers of beans from Mr. C. W. Phillips, of Daresbury, Mr. Birch, of Higher Walton, and Mrs. Forster, of Laira street.

The above annual meeting of assistants was held on Wednesday night at the Cocoa 'ROMDS, Sankey-street, at eight o'clock, when a fairly good attendance of agents was present. The chair was occupied by the general President, Mr. Joseph Hill, when the business relating both to collectors, and the assured benefits, was discussed previous to the annual Staff Association meeting to be held in Manchester. The Warrington District being represented by Messrs. Ashcroft, Superintendents Gorton and Dolan. After the meeting was over, Mr. Ashcroft, superintendent, was asked to perform a very pleasing duty. in presenting a very handsome silver cruet stand, to Mr. T. Gorton, Secre- tary of the Staff Association,in honour of his marriage. and for the admirable way in which he had performed the duties of Secretaryship, for ths Association. Pleas- ing remarks were made by Messrs. Ashcroft, Hill, Jenning and Dolan, in fact all the meeting was unani- mous in wishing Mr, and Mrs. Gorton, long life, health and prosperity. "PREMIER" CYCLES at 9 guineas for Best Grade, unsurpassed.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt. Clergy Daughters' School. The anniversary of the Clergy Daughters' School, formerly a Warrington institution, but now of Darks: Dale,"was, owing to an outbreak of scarlet fever at the school, held at Warrington on Wednesday, the Bishop of Chester presiding. The Rector (the Rev. Canon Willis) presented the annual report, which showed that the number of girls in the institution had increased from 79 to 81. The Headmistress spoke in vigorous terms of the healthy condition of- the girls. Unfortunately there had been an, outbreak of scarlet fever during the past 12 months, 18 cases having occurred. These were of a mild character and so far as could be ascertained infection was carried from outside. All the patients were now convalescent. With regard to financial matters £12,000 was still re- quired for the purchase and alteration of the Darley Dale premises, against which had to be set the value of the Warrington premises and land. Another £l,OOO would prolably be required for the erection of a sanatorium.—Canon Penrhyn reported that the retiring members of the committee, who were all eligible for re-election, were Canon Turner, Lord Egerton of Talton, Mr. T. H. Lyon, Rev W. Stanton, Rev. J. A. Ruston, Mr. P. D. Christie, Rev. Canon Spooner, Rev. Canon Matthew, Captain Willington and Colonel Buckton. The retiring members of the committee were reelected and the Rev 4. Cr. 13. Thurston and W. J. Bradford were elected to fill vacancies caused by death of the Rev. R. Greenall and the resignation of Canon Stowell. The Rev. G.B. Thurston was appointed secretary in the place of Canon Penrhyn who resigned the office.

Alleged Unlawful Wounding. , At the Warrington Police Clad on Thursday,Arthur Pratt, labourer, 7, Pitt street,was charged with unlaw- fully wounding Dorothy- Carolino. Blasdale. Superin- tendent Monaghan stated that, the prisoner formerly lodged with the prosecutrix at 7; Pitt street. About a fartnight ago prisoner again came Jo the house for lodgings, and Mrs. Blasdale told lim she could not accommodate him unless he was 'willing to sleep on the sofa. Yesterday afternoon the other lodgers were out, and Mrs. Blasdale was writing a letter in the front room. Prisoner went behind her,and it was alleged drew something sharp (presumably a razor) across her throat. She struggled, and managed to get int ) the kitchen, but prisoner followed her, and again attacked 1 her and cut her about the face. Eventually she escaped through the back kitchen window, and was attended at the Infirmary. She lost a quantity of blood, and had to have eleven stitches in her face and throat. Shortly before five o'clock on Wednesday night prisoner came to the police station and said he had cut a woman's throat. lie was detained, and Police-constables Leonard and Hamblett made inquiries. He (Snperintendent Monaghan) asked for a remand [or a week to see how Mrs. Blasdale went on. She *as in a bad state that morning.—Prisoner was accordingly remanded until Thursday next. •

VOLUNTEER ANNUAL CAMP.

To-day's Departure.

To-day (Saturday) the members of the Ist Volunteer Battalion Prince of Wales' Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment) leave Warrington fo• their annual camp training. Their destination is Windmill Hill, Salisbury Plain, where they will remain under canvas for a fort- night. The first to leave will be the Alounted Infantry, Cyclist Companies, officers, 6:c., as detailed up to 100 non-commissioned officers and men, who under the command of Captain Crosfield, will parade at 9.15 in the morning and proceed by an early train which will convey lthe officers' charges. The remainder of the Battalion will fall in at the Drill Hall at 9.15 p.m. in marching but without helmets, and purist) i to Bank Qaay Station where they will entrain at 7.30. The annual inspection will take place this year while the Battalion is encamped but the date is not yet fixed. Owing to the distance to be travelled only those men who can attend for the full per:od can be present at inspection, and the Commanding Officer is confident therefore, that for the credit of the Battalion, every man will do his utmost to attend camp this year in order that the required number may be present on inspection parade Company Commanders in camp will be, ILI., Captain P. N. Avery; A, Captain A. B. Layton; B. Captain, C. F. Carey ;O, Major P. I‘l. Appleton; D, Lieutenant R. EL Lapage ; E, Lieutenant E. A. Connor ; F, Captain H. G. Roberts ; G, Lieutenant J. T. Picton ; H, Captain R. Smith; I, Captain G. R. Crosfield and Cyclists, Lieu- tenant H. Owen.

A 4uantity of Tobacco has been given to the old men inmates of the Downham Workhouse, and by the express desire of the donor two old women who have a partiality for the fragrant weed are to be included in the distribu- tion.

EDUCATION UNDER CROMWELL.

Lively Correspondence Between Revs. H. Dunnico and E. C. E. Carleton.

SOME ANCIENT HISTORY-CHALLENGE TO A PUBLIC DEBATE.

[To THE EDITOR OF THE EXAMINER.] Sir,-1 have pleasure in inclosing copy of a corres- pondence that has taken place between the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton and myself. The rev, gentleman has I intimated his intention to publish same but as I do not wish to undertake any additional work (I am on holiday) I desire now to make the only statement I shall make, prior to my return. The statement Mr. Carleton for some unknown reason objects to was made in a sermon on Sunday June 4th and leads thus. " There was a period of English History known as the commonwealth period. Among many distinctions lof that period it made popular education among its chief concerns and made a large grant each year in support of it."l was at that time endeavouring to prove that the claim of the Church of England to having aroused the conscience of the state in matters of educa- tion was not in accordance with fact. That 1181 years previously a nonconformist and a 'puritan had done this in the person of Oliver Cromwell. Now this is a fact of history which no amount of quibbling can explain away. This was apparently a startling piece of news for Mr. Carleton, he had never heard of such a thing (this fact in itself proves my critic to be an authority on English History) and was about to deny it, but, discovering his mistake he sought to minimise its importance by slandering a man who did more for this country in a few brief years than the Archbishops and Bishops of all times. This appears to be a favourite method of controversy with my opponent. He has meted out the same treatment to myself and others only a few weeks ago he stated that I had "honoured" my flock by teaching them that which if put into practice would secure their conviction in a criminal court. Although I have drawn his attention to that statement he has not been courteous enough to either' explainlor apologise. One does not feel dis- posed to be lenient with an opponent of this kind. Mr. Carleton's version of the Commonwealth period is a baseless fabrication and I am prepared to prove that Cromwell was movable and intellectually superior to his predecessors or successors. Oliver Cromwell was the first of England's rulers after Alfred to discern the importance of education. During the Protectorate great efforts were made for the enlightenment of the people, schools sprang up in every direction. Universities flourished as never before but when the black days of the Stuart re-action came all this was swept away and 2,000 of the holiest and most learned clergy were ejected from their livings. But even admitting that Mr. Carleton's charges are true the worse that one could say about Cromwell would be that he took the ill-gotton gains of the privileged few and used them for the good of the oppressed many. To-day we have a Government returned to power on a false issue and a Government that has fraudulently used that power in bolstering up the priest and the parson, the brewer and the Chinese slave owner at the expense of poor and struggling masses. The puny ' criticisms of men like Mr. Carleton fall harmlessly upon the man who stands out as the embodiment of all that is best and noblest in the English spirit—the typical I Englishman reuewed and transfigured by the spirit of God.—Yeurs sincerely HERBERT DUNNICO.

Holy Trinity Vicarage, Warrington. 2Gth Juno. 1905.

Dear Sir,—ny attention is drawn to a statement made in a sermon preached by you and reported in the Warrington "Examiner" of .June 10th list, It is as follows:

"There was a period of English History known as the Commonwealth period. Among many distinctions of that period, it made Popular Education among its chief concerns, and made a large grant each year in support of it."

May I say that I shall be much obliged ir you will kindly give me your authority for this sta*tement, 1 mean an authoritative reference that may be verified, as the matter is one of much interest and importance.— Believe me, faithfully yours, E. C. E. CARLETON.

The Rev. H. Minnie°. .._.-

July 3rd, 1905. Dear Sir,—Absence from home with its inevitable accumulation of correspondence has prevented me replying to your letter of the 28th earlier. The state- ment you refer to may be verified by consulting any reliable history of the Cromwellian period and the efforts put forth by that great man in order to promote a scheme of general gratuitous education ; however as you ask for "an authoritative reference that may be verified," I beg to say that such a reference may be found in "State Intervention in English Education." a Short History from the Earliest Times" down to 1833. The author is J. E. G. Montmorency, 8.A., LL.B. of St. Peter's College, Cambridge, and of the Middle Temple, Barrister at Law. The sentence (page 101) reads as follows :—This allocation to education of the annual sum of £20,000 preceded by 181 years the first grant made by Parliament under the modem educational system." May I take this opportunity of saying how much I regret the tone of the article that appeared this month your Church Magazine. Whoever the writer may be (I pass over as being unworthy of notice the references to the Baptist Minister and his flock) he certainly lays himself open to the charge of inexcusable ignorance on the one hand, or a deliberate perversion of the truth on the other, either of which is presumably due to bigotry.—Yours sincerely HERBERT DUNNICO. P.S.—I shall be pleased to know whether you are satisfied with this reference.

Holy Trinity Vicarngo, 12th July, 1905. Dear Sir,—l regret that I have not been able to reply before to your note of the 3rd inst.owing to the pressure of many engagements. As I have not the book to which you refer in my possession (" State intervention in English education") and as lam not acquaintei with Mr. Montmorency as an authority, perhaps you will kindly give me the references that I have no doubt he himself furnishes as the basis of his statement.

May I say that at the time to which you apparently refer (1649), the Parliament of England did .not exist. There were a few men—some 40 or 50 I think—who professed to act in the name of Parliament ; but the great body of the peoples' representatives had been turned away from the doors of the House of Commons by Colonel Pride and his two regiments, because those who controlled the army at that time felt they could not rely upon their votes. When people like these give away other people's money, I am not able to gauge the measure of the sacrifice or assign the motive that dictated the generosity. If you will however give me the exact date of the vote—year and month—l will 'endeavour to look the matter up in the records of Parliament as I have opportunity. Certainly there is, believe, no Educational Institution in the country or School or College that is preserved to us as dating from the period of the Commonwealth.—l am sir, yours faithfully E. C. E. CARLETON. The Rev. H. Dunnico.

_ Holy Trinity Vicarage, \Carrington, July 19th 1905. Dear Sir,—May I say that my inquiry as to the date of the grant for education under the Commonwealth is one that, I think, there need be no delay about. How- ever, as I do not wish to give you any unnecessary trouble, and as I think I know what it is that your statement refers to, I will venture to deal with the matter without further parley. I have already pointed out that this' so-called grant was made not by Parliament but by a body that had been careful to exclude by force the great body of the people's representatives from having any voice in the affairs of Government. . . .

Further the grant was made not out of any funds raised by legitimate taxation, but out of funds raised by the confiscation "of all parsonages, tithes, oblations, obventions, &c. " the proceeds of which were to be held by trustees, who were to pay out of it what Parliament thought desirable for the "maintenance of preaching ministers, the advancement of learning and other pions uses." It is this Act to which I think you refer, passed on the Bth of June 1649. It was necessary I suppose, after the Clergy and Schoolmasters were turned out of their emoluments that those who were put in their places under the Commonwealth should have some pro- vision made for their maintenance,and this was the object of the Act. Whether education was likely to be benefited by a proceeding of this kind, I am content to leave to the judgment of others. We know from other sources how education stood in the country at this time. The records of the Oxford Visitation may be seen in Walker's " Sufferings of the Clergy." The fact is education under Cromwell fared no better than the other great interests of the country; and how these fared may be gathered from the following summary that I have taken with slight alteration. from a letter of Disraeli to Lord Lyndhurst:—This Govern- ment established courts more infamous than the Star Chamber in every county, with power of fining, sequestrating, imprisoning, and corporally punishing all who opposed or even murmured against their decrees; and under the plea of "malignancy" they avenged their private hatreds, and seized for their private gain any ' estates or property to which they took a fancy. They enlarged the laws' of high treason, so that they com- prehended verbal offences and even intentions: They voted trial hy jury a breach of parliamentary privilege. They sequestrated nearly one half of the goods and chattels of the nation, and at least one-half of its, rents and revenues; and in seven years raised the taxation of the country from eight hundred thousand pounds per annum to seven millions per annum. This Government had the honour of inventing the excise, applying the impost even to provisions and the common necessaries

of life. They became at length so barefaced in their extortions, that one morning they openly divided three hundred thousand pounds amongst themselves, and settled an annuity of four pounds a day on each of their number (see Disraeli's Vindication of the Con- stitution).

The Government of Cromwell throughout was simply a military dictatorship and the army was perhaps the only institution that flourished under his rule. The notion that there was liberty in tho country either civil or religious, is a myth. As I have given you an opportunity of making a correction of astatement.publicly made by you,which you have not availed yourself of, I propose sending this correspondence to the Press.-4 am yours faithfully. E. C. E. CARLETON. The Rev. H. Dunnico.

July 20th. Dear Sir,—Your letter of the 12th came to hand during my absence in London. Since my return I have had more important business to attend to than that of sending out information every intelligent person ought to be in possession of. Moreover, as you delayed acknowledging a favour I did for you for some ten days, I do not see that you have any cause for complaint. Your statement at to " there being no need for delay " in sending you information your previous letters prove you to have been absolutely ignorant of, savours very much of comic opera. Such methods of controversy are more in keeping with those of p >Utica] tricksters than those of men claiming to be in the episcopal suc- cession. Lot me however hasten to assure you that I had no intention of continuing a correspondence with one whose audacity and bigotry and insolence are in inverse ratio to his knowledge of the subject under discussion. If proof in support of this statement is required I need only refer you to the article in your own magazine and your letter of the 12th inst. So far as your last letter was concerne4 I can only say that such a misleading and inaccurate letter was never penned by any man whose mind had not been ivarpad by narrow bigotry. lam delighted to learn that you have decided to send a copy of this correspondence to the press, at the same time your eagerness to do so at this stage and without even asking my permission only confirms the opinion I had previously formed of you. I need hardly inform you that I ant at the present time engaged in a discussion with one who is in every respect better fitted and more capable of discussing this subject that yourself and who is an opponent lan able to admire and respect. Moreover as I leave Warrington on Monday next until early September I do not feel called upon to undertake any additional cor- respondence. In fact I question whether a controversy with an opponent such as you appear to be is desirable under any circumstances. Still lest anyone sbould think that I am not able to defend every statement you have thought fit to question I beg to stato that .1 am pre- pared to meet you in public debate. I mike this offer because a newspaper controversy absorbs more time than I can spare. The debate to take place in the Parr Hall, to extend over three consecutive nights and on dates suitable to both parties. I am willing to hold myself responsible for half the expenzes. I trust you will publish this letter along with the rest.—Yours truly,

HERBERT DUNNICO. P.S.—I make this offer purely in the public interest. If you state your case as you understand it I wilt state ours as I understand it. Toe public then can form their own opinion.

Holy Trinity Vicarage, Wirrington. 21st July, 1905. Dear Sir,—As you seem unwilling to give our corres- pondence poblication, I will allow the matter to stand over for a few d ays. Meanwhile perhaps you can hit upon some other plan for removing the wrong impression that, I conceive,'has been given the public by your statement. The method of having a public dis- cussion on so narrow an issue as the meaning of an Act passed by the Commonwealth Parliament in June 1649 is simply ridiculous. It is as if a man, who was challenged with putting in circulation a bank note of doubtful genuineness, should propose to the objector to fight him for it, instead of taking the obvious course of having ta.e note tested.

Of course if you have no Om to props e for dis- abusing the public mind in this matter, I must do what I can without your assistance and without printing your letters as they stand.—Yours faithfully, E. C. E. CARLETON. .

Lie Rev. H. Dunnico.

July 21, 1905. Dear Sir,—Your letter of the 21st is another charac- teristic attempt to take an ugfair advantage. You know perfectly well I have not raised any objection to the publication of thecorrespondence. In my last letter I distinctly stated that I was pleased to learn that you had decided to send a copy of same to the Press. I did suggest that as you had asked privately for certain information I was under no obligation to supply you with, it was to say the least only courteous to ask per- mission before sending such to the Press. I now ask you to publish the whole of it, if you do not dc.) so I shall. The statement you have challeaged still remains intact, and I deeply regret that a gentleman in your position should put forth such narrow-mindad and paltry criticism. So far as my offer to debate is concerned. let me add that I do not desire to limit the debate to so narrow an issue ; I wish to discuss the whole subject from a citizen's point of view. I desire an opportunity of telling you on a public platform that your recent article reproduced in the columns of the local press is an insolent and impudent slander, and that it is abso- lately void of truth.—Yours truly, HERBERT DUNNICO. Rev. E. C. E. Carleton.

MR. F. NEILSON.

Politician, Author, Dramatist and

Globe-Trotter.

Francis Neilson, the Liberal Labour candidate for the Newport Division of Shropshire, wh ) has been ad- dressing Warrington audiences this week, is an in- teresting personality. He begin life in Birkenhead, was "raised "in Shropshire, and has since travelled pretty well all over the continent and America, most of the time, as he humourously says, making money for other people. Now he is busy making a reputa- tiou in the fascinating sphere of politics, and th ise of our readers who have heard Mr. Neilson will hope and believe that success lies before him. Few men have a better platform manner, a pleasanter voice, or the ability to endow political speech-making with a livelier interest. In appearance he is tall and athletic looking, clean-shaven, dark-haired and with an eye and month that smile in unison. Mr. Neilson's career may be said to have com- menced when at the age of 19 he voyaged to America on fortune bent. Riches did not exactly shower upon him when he landed. His first job was in a New York fruit commission warehouse, where he had to work from 12 midnight until 8 o'clock the following evening-20 hours a thy —and sleep on the premises. Since then Mr. Neilson has assiduously advocated an 8 hours day! From New York Mr. Neilson travelled all over the United States, Mexico, Canada, and the Continent, working at various jobs, including rail- roading. After many experiences he at last became a journalist in America. Then he met with Dion Baucicault and became the latter's private secretary for the last three years of his life. Through the great actor and playwright, Mr. Neilson became interested in dramatic enterprise and obtained the post of stage director to Charles Frehman. In 1897 he returned to England with William Gillette's "Secret Service" Company,which made the first real American success in this country, while he also staged the first performance in this county of "Sherlock Holmes." Later, Mr. Neilson went into Royal Opera as stage director and managed affairs for 4 years, leaving opera to take up politics, which he had always taken an interest in, even while in America. At one time he was at work with George Douglas Blown, author of "The house with the green shutters," and made a study of South African affairs from the commencement of the British administration to the war. When he saw the nation rushing headlong to Protection under the guidance of Mr. Chamberlain he determined to do all he could to bring the people back to a sane view of things, and was delighted when he received an invitation from the county in which he had spent the early part of his life to contest the Newport Division. The Progressive Party soon realised the value of Mr. Neilson's experi,-nces in organisation, and readily availed themselves of his services in bye-elections. During the last 18 months Mr. Neilson has helped in the working and organisation of 13 bye-elections, and looks back with pride on the fact that he was associa- ted with four of the biggest victories the Progressive forces have yet won. In his opinion, Oswestry and Whitby were the two biggest performances. Like a good candidate should be, Mr. Neilson is confident of his own success at Newport, and has every reason to hope that he will succeed. in ousting the sitting mem- ber, Col. Kenyon Slaney, M.P. A. word in reference to Mr. Neilson's literary' and dramatic achievements will not be out of place. here. ' He has already published a novel, "Madam Bohemia," and a musical drama on the myths of the North American Indiana, entitled " Manabozo " which was written for Anton Seidl. Several plays have also come from Mr. 'Neilson's pen, and during the winter he expects to publish another novel. While in America he found time to produce a number of plays and operettas, one entitled "Prince Ananias " being very well known in the United States.Politieal articles also come within Mr. Neilson's province, so that altogether he is an uncommonly busy man of many parts.

A TRAMP ABROAD. IMPRESSIONS OF WARRINGTON AND WIDNES. "THIS POISONOUS HELL-TOWN." The day was terribly hot, and I got badly burned through the wearing of only a cap. I met a good many men going and coming alorr, the road. I passed through Cad ishead, and finally the town of \Carrington hove into sight. It was not a town that made a dark cloud in the air, as did the most of Lancashire towns

A train came out along the road about a mile out from the town, and I boarded it gladly. The eleven miles walk in the glare of the sun had tired me a bit. ‘Varrington is a picturesque, clean-aired, pleasant town, and it owts a policeman who possesses wit. For when I asked hint as to where I should put up and where the best place was to get a meal, he re- galed me with many humorous impressions concerning myself. Among other things he told me was that must have been a pugilist. He had had a great deal to do with pugilists, he said, and he thought that if 1 trained down a bit I would be all right. He was a stout block of a man xvith an egotistical voice, and he went on to give his views of life generally as he stood at the crossing regulation.' the traffic of the main street of Warrington. I gave him a cigar, and passed on to get the benefit of his expert advice as to the town.

That night I went to the theatre, where the most luri I kind of a melodrama was being played. But the somewhat vivid charm of the piece was not strong enough to keep me awake. The day had tired me. _

THE WONDERFUL CANAL.

The next morning I was up bright and early to start along the road to Liverpool. But it occurred to me that it would be a good idea to go to Latchford Locks to see the Ship Canal. Here were the locks. And the wonderful canal was stretching for miles under the light of the sun. And then it occurred to me that it would be a good idea to walk along its banks to Liverpool. As I was looking round the immense locks It coasting steam schooner of over a hundred tons entered from the higher level. She was on her way from Manchester to Liverpool, and was called the Bedlormie—and before I knew where I was Captain Waring, the superintendent of the locks, spoke to her captain and I was aboard. Going down the canal on a vessel was the best way of all to see it. And I settled myself comfortably on a plank seat on the bridge with the captain. All thoughts of walking had now vanished. I would glide easily and quietly and lazily to Liverpool. Or rather to Ilistham. For the canal ended at Easthara on the Mersey, twenty and a half miles away. There were no more locks to descend. The great canal was now on a level with the mouth of the Mersey and with the far ocean. Of all the ways of travelling, travelling by canal is the easiest and the safest and most beautiful. There is neither jar nor rock nor any sense of untoward motion. You glide easily and serenel3 along. The country passes before you with the soft ease of a dream. You are at once going and you are still and at rest. It is motion in stillness. The world passes before your eves with a slow and serene and strange enchantment. The boat moves as move the clouds. You go along as in a dream. You have time to think upon the things you see. They calmly pass before you in the fullness and their roundness. You see the beginning and the middle and the end of them. You are under the spell of the softest and gentlest and most perfect mood of the wondrous all-god motion. And you sail calmly along in still quietude. And fields slowly arise, and full reveal themselves, and pass. And the grasses and the trees and the flowers softly and slowly reveal themselves. And towns slowly arise above the banks in the distance. The sounds that come are AS soft sounds in stillness. You go gently along in stillness. You are held in the spell of Motion's most beautiful and most magical mood. How enthrall- ing and strange and soft and quiet it all is. And the Journey is at an end. And it is still as if you were at the beginning. A sense of rest is with you. The journey has been as the soft passing of a cloud. There is not even the faintest sense of a strain upon you. A picture has passed before you. That is all. -

-- ----- FOR LUST OF GAIN. Widnes appeared. It rose slowly up in the north. west. A dark dreadful picture in the midst of the splendid, wonderful day. An infernal place of belching, poisoning fumes, where people were being slowly murdered in the name of science and the name of greed. The snakish, poisonous, murder-fumes wreathed and slimed slowly upwards, defiling and degrading the bright- ness. This poisonous hell-town rose up in the north- west. Yonder, from the vast chimneys, arose monstrous snakes more deadly than the snake-monsters of the dim and long-gone past. Truly, life end the world changed but little. Man had evolved in the midst of the horror of a reptile environment. And the snake-monsters of the long-gone past were gone. But man had evoked them again here in this Widnes. In the old times the slimy immense monsters but slew man. But these monsters arising before me slew even the grass and the trees. They arose and spurned, defiling and degrading the brightness of the splendid day. These horrid spawn of the lust of greed and the foolish, burrowing blind thing of egotism that man calls Science. Better would i; have been if this Widnes—this poisonous hell-town—were blotted out. Science! What have you done for man save degrade him and take away from him his innate and native power ? The horn of the Bedlormie blew and the swing bridges softly swung open as if by magic to let us pass. And the dark, dread blot faded away. And here were fresh and green fields. The wonderful suspension bridge of Runcorn I It appeared—a structure of delicate and interlacing lines that seemed to rest of itself in the bright summer air. And we went on till we could see off from us be faint yellow water of the Mersey. It ran in a line with the still waters of the grand and wonderful canal. Here to the south-east was the River Weaver. The canal seemed now to lie in the midst of moving waters. And off before us was the widening mouth of the River Mersey. Before us a great and mighty vessel moved slowly along the canal. We had come upon her in our journey. We could see the men standing upon her deck. She had come from a part of the world far distant, and again she was out-bound. An immense, stupendous vessel going slowly. And here was Eastham. Here was the beginning of the great canal. And yonder over the broad width of the river was the town of Liverpool.

BART KENNEDY, In the" Daily Mail." IN DEFENCE OF WIDNES. [To the Editor of the " Daily Mail.") Sir,—Who is Mr. Bart Kennedy? That is the ques- tion asked by the natives of Widnes since the A ppe9 ranee of Mr. Kennedy's article in Monday's " Daily Mail." A warm welcome awaits the man who describes our native heath ,as a" poisonous hell town." Such an epithet could but emanate from a sordid mind. The town has been libelled, and grossly libelled. It is time such writers were made to" toe the line." It lis time our municipal authorities made an example of men who make such unfounded statements, as ranee harm may be done to the progress of such towns. "Widnes may not some years ago have been a "thing of beauty," but now it can hold its own with any town insouth-west Lancashire. There are several towns in England to.day which are far worse than Widnes. From the Ship Canal a most pleasant view is obtained of one portion of ‘Vidnes; that portion is most pleasing to the eye—the magnificent and spacious promenade and fairly decent property. This, then, is what Mr. Bart Kennedy gazed upon when he was on the Ship Canal, and one is at a loss to understand the origin or cause of his absurd aneuncharitable effusions. Just over the water are beautiful gardens, laid out on the promenade. Then our park is but a mile from the chemical works, and yet he asserts that not a blade of grass can be seen in the town. Nature is seen at its best close to the spot where Mr. Bart Kennedy framed his astounding untruths concern- ing our town. There are many readers of the "Daily Mail "in Widnes, and we feel greatly annoyed and quite angry with your correspondent. We can offer Mr. Batt Kennedy numerous luxuries. We can show him where his remarks are erroneous in their entirety. We will point out to him blades of grass, and after he has con- cluded his inspection of Widnes we can guarantee him repose in as nice a little cemetery as one would wish to rest in from his weary labours.

D. J. D. APPLETON.

Widnes.

STOCKTON HEATH.

-- Tø E CIARDEN SOCIETY PICSIC.—The members of the. Stockton Heath Garden Society and their wives and lady friends on Saturday afternoon visited Birchdale, the residence of their President, Mr. Vera B. Davies, the guests altogether numbering about 300. The beauti- ful grounds, which are in excellent condition were much admired, the inspection affording considerable pleasure. In addition to Mr. and Mrs. Vera Davies, who did all they could to minister to the happiness of the guests, there were present the Rev. H. V. and Mrs. Pigot, the Rev. C. A. IW. Evans, Mr. A. Fawcett (secretary of the society) and Mrs. Fawcett, Mr. Charles Plinston (treasurer), and the following members of he com- mittee :—Messrs. F. Timperley, W. Timperley, IV. H. Woodcock, Vernon, Moore and Anslow. Games, includ- ing cricket and bowls, were engaged in, and a number of prizes were distributed to the successful competitors. Excellent refreshments were provided and a pleasant afternoon and evening were spent.

A WORD TO THE WISE.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Rev. H. Dannleo's Anniversary.

Special services were held on Sunday in celebration of the Rev. H. Dunnico's third anniversary as Pastor of the Golborne-street Baptist Church. A large congrega- tion assembled in the morning, when the Pastor preached on the duties and obligations of church members. In the afternoon the church was well filled, when the half- yearly prize distribution took place in connection with the adult Bible Class, above 100 lbooks being given out. In the evening many were unable to gain admis- sion. Basing his remarks on the words, "Ye are the salt of the earth but if the silt has lost the savour . . . it is henceferth good for nothing but to be cast out and trodden under the foot of men." Mr. Dunnico delivered a sermon on the mission and the purpose of the Christian Church and its place in national life. Speak- ing upon the Christian Church's failure and dishonour the preacher crit;cised the church of to-day very severely. The steady decrease on church attendance, and its failure to supply men with what their deepest needs required was to the church's discredit. The men of to- day were not less religions than their fathers, even though they attended church less regularly. The cause lay inside the church. It revealed so little of the -.Master's spirit of helpfulness and pity, and men wera beginning to see through the hollow hypocrises and shams of many so-called pillars in the church. When the church became tilled with the spirit of Christ and power of the Holy Ghost her courts would then be crowded by the poor, the weary and the oppressed, instead of by a few sleek well-fed prosperous Christians who paid more heed to creeds, sacraments and their own little services than to the perishing world the Master died to save. Mr. Dunnico will not occupy his pulpit again until September.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. Vigorous Address by Mr. J. L. Paton.

There was a crowded attendance at an open meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society which was held on Sunday. The Rev. J. Von so presided and in introduc- ing the speaker, Mr. J. L. Paton, High Master of the Manchester Grammar School, said they had every reason to feel proud of Mr. Paton as he was one of the comparatively few Nonconformists who had distinguished themselves in university life and had won for themselves positiorD, of rank, res- ponsibility and influence in the country. (Hear, hear.) —Mr. Paton, at the outset of his address, gave an excel. lent account of the story contained in Shakespeare's "Merchant of Venice" of the wooing of Portia. The object of the man in business, be said, was the attain- ment of wealth, and his whole life was given up to that pursuit. The man who spent his time endeavour- ing to" spot winners was as truly a worshipper of ' gold as the motorist who rushed past with his dust and smell. Greed for gold was a false and empty idea Of life. After all what could wealth give us ? Wealth made us cowards on the brink of the last cold stream which we all must cross. There were others whose idea of life was not so sordid as those who sold their souls for wealth but even they had not the right idea of life. They asked that life should give them what they deserved hut somehow they never got from life what they thought they were entitled to. That was a false philosophy of life, and the persons who adopted it were always discontented grumblers. There was, however, .another in our midst, and He asked that His followers should deny themselves, and take up their crosses and follow Him. It seemed a hard ideal of life for the young men to give and hazard all that they had, and were and could become. It was, however, the true life of love, and if we followed it we must not ask for reward, we must not ask for what we deserved, but must live for the joy of serving Christ, so that in the end we would be transfigured with glory, as we would be with Christ in God. (Applause).—During the afternoon Miss P. Houghton sang with excellent effect " With verdure clad" and "Thy King," Mr. Payton presiding at the organ. Mr. E. Wallington also took part in the meet- ing. The President welcomed one new member, the total number now being 455. A vote of sympathy and condolence was sent to Mr. T. Woods, one of the oldest members who had during the week tosthis wife after a long and lingering sickness.

William Ridley, who was described as a "gentle- man," was fined £5 at Holt, Norfolk, for assaulting a female visitor by kissing her. As the woman was sitting on the beach at Clpy, the man put his arm round her waist and kissed her several times.

Dr. Corbett, who is said to be the only blind man who ever took the degree of doctor of music in England, was appointed professor of music at the Nottingham Blind Institution on Wednesday. He comes from Wellington, Shropshire, and has held the position of organist at Holy Trinity, Bournemouth, and All Saints', Derby. Mr. George J. Gould and his wife and son were severely horsewhipped by a. Swiss shepherd, whose cart was upset by the motor-csr in which the American millionaire was driving, in the neighbourhood of Kass- nacht.

THE OVERCROWDING OF THE WORKFIOUSE.

THE BOARD OF GUARDIANS AND THE DIFFICULTY.

ViEWS OF MR. JEINER FUST.

For some time the Warrington Board of Guar lians have been faced with a serious difficulty in regard to the congested state of the Workhouse. The Medical Officer of Health dealing with the question in his report says : " General overcrowding in the hospital —more particularly on the female side and male lunatic block —lO2 sleepers on female side only ought to be 95 —and on the male side 85 sleepers and accom- modation for 94 patients." Under the head of " Recommendations " the report read " On 'January 2nd also I referred to the overcrowding of the hospital amt the Guardians have discussed the matter but nothing has ly)en done. I should like to call the Guardians' attention t ) this matter again—George Braide, melical officer." In reply to one of the queries set forth in the " Half-yearly statement of the Medical Officer for the Workhouse'' is the following entry : " The h )spital generally has been overcrowded during the past six months." At the interim meeting of the Board, which was held on Wednesday, the question was again under discussion. Mr. Billinge presided, and there were also present : Mrs. Clay, Miss Broadbent, Rev. T. ft. Matthews, Drs. Murray and Patterson, and Messrs. NV. Hickman. A. J. Jolley, S. Davies, T. H. Ashcroft, R. Forshaw, G. Thompson, G. Mather, J. Brookes, W. Flannigan, R. Bolton, M. T. Seton, J. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (workhouse master), and A. Wright (superintendent of Padgate Schools.) Mr. Jenner Feat (Local Government Board Inspector) was also present. The Cif AIRMAN said that the question of the proposed alteration of the old hospital with a view to increasing the accommodation had been very carefully considered. The premises were some days previously visited by one of the Local Government Bqard architects, and Mr. Jenner Feat and several members of the Board, to confer upon the proposal. Mr. JENNER FEST said he was pleased to have that opportunity of being present. He had hoped that the question of the extension of the Workhouse was settled when the Board arrived at the decision to erect a pavilion for the accommodation of the infirm. Unfortunately during the discussion and necessary preliminaries which took place the pressure of the rates made itself felt and there WaS a very strong feeling throughout the country that the pressure was almost more than the ratep tyers could bear. Not un-naturally therefore, the Guardians, feeling the pressure of the rates, cast about them to see whether or not there was a means of dealing with the question of overcrowd- ing without the expenditure of so large a sum as a new building would involve, and the suggestion was made that they should alter the old Infirmary in order to ; accommodate the class for whom the extra accommoda- lion was more p trticularly required. Their architect (Mr. Owen) drew up preliminary plans in order to ascertain the feeling of the Local Government Board but Mr. Kitchen, the Local Govemmeut Board archi- tect, seemed to have taken a somewhat unfavourable view especially as the alterations would cost £2500. Ile and Mr. Kitchen had inspected the old infirmary in order that they could better judge its condition and speak as to the desirability of spending money upon it. Mr. Kitchen had come to the conclusion that, the building was not worth the expense which would be necessary to make it suitable for imbeciles or the aged and infirml in the manner pro- posed. He was influenced by the knowledge that if the extension of the new infirmary became necessary and that building was completed occording to the plans it would involve the pulling down of the old infirmary. Thore was another point which the Board must remember. If they borrowed money to spend it upon the old infirmary they would only be able to borrow it for a short time, but if they borrowed money for the erection of a new pavilion they would get 30 years in which to repay it. The proper use of the old infirmary was in connection with the main bonding and to relieve the pressure felt dur'ng the winter months and at exceptional periods. Assuming that the Board agreed with the view that the old building was not worth spending the money upon was there any alternative scheme? There were: to erect a pavilion or to complete the new building by the erection of two half blocks. The latter could be done without inter- fering with the existing building and would give a con- siderable amount of increased accommodation for any class of inmate. That, however, he was afraid wool(' cost considerably more than a new pavilion which the Board originally decided to put up. He assumed that it was admitted that an extension of some kind of the existing accommodation was necessary. Various arguments put forward to show that was not so. It was said that the Workhouse was certifiA to hold so many, but it had never been called upon to accommodate that number. It was difficult to conceive that a person who brought forward that argu- ment belie -ed in it himself. If people would learn where the accommodation was vacant and regulate their applications accordingly there would he no diffi- culty, but when ten able-bodied men applied for admis- sion they could not be told that there was plenty of room in the female imbecile ward. (Hear, hear.) It was suggested that the difficulty could be mei by the extension of the system of out-relief. They might stave off a few cases by that means but where it had been tried it had been found that the reduction effected in the number of inmates was very small, and it was doubtful whether it was permanent. He was, therefore, afraid they must dismiss from their minds any idea of being able to deal with the question in that way. He was sorry some of the opponents of the scheme were not present when he and ?'fr. Kitchen visited the premises. He was present, and he would be surprised i f that gentleman did not vote for an extension. Mr. [-tick:v.l.x asked what it would cost to take down the interior walls of the old infirmary. Mr. SEGAR OWEN replied that that would necessitate other work being done which would make the total cost about £l,OO. .

Mr. AsHCROFT : lam one of the opponents of the scheme but I was not asked to be present. . .

Mr. J. C. surros : Yon were invited to come but were away from home I sent a clerk to inform you. Mr. HICKMAN said that on account of the shortness of the notice he could not be present when Mr. Jenner Fast made his visit owing to business engage- ments. He had taken an active interest in oppos- ing the larger scheme for reasons which he thought were good. He felt that before they embarked upon a building scheme they should utilise the premises they already possessed as far as was possible. He said all along a huildingscherne could be deferred and for that reason he suggested the alteration °tithe old infirmary, but, however, he did not intend to be en advocate of the spending of £2,500 or even £1,500 but thought the work should be done as cheaply as possible, and with all due reference to Mr. Owen he did not think the work would cost £1,501 Mr. FORS IT AW : What would be the increased accommodation if the alterations were carried out. Mr. HICKMAN : Fifty nine. __ • _ .

The CHA I RMAN : There would not he any increased accommodation if Mr. Hickman's suggestion were carried out. Mr. HicxmAx : There is bound to be an increased ' accommodation as you would increase the amount of space at your disposal. Mr. THOMPSON said ha believed the accommodation at the Workhouse was 591 whereas the greatest number they had had to accommodate that year was 583 and the least 462. The old infirmary could be made suitable for the accommodation of the aged and infirm and would relieve the pressure on the Workhouse. Mr. PortsHAW : I think Mr. Thompson has over- looked the question of classification. Mr. DAVIES said they had three schemes before them. The first was to erect a new building at a cost of £B,OOO, the second to alter the old infirmary and the third to ex- tend the new infirmary.He thoura the alteration to the old infirmary would be a patched up job and altogether unsatisfactory—(hear, hear,)—and to his 'ailed that scheme entirely fell to the ground. He did not think there was a single member of the Board who would honestly support the spending of £2,500 on a patched up job such as that would be. The first scheme would be more satisfactory and would meet all their requirements. He had never opposed that scheme land all he had asked was an extension of time as it was well known extensive schemes were being deferred all over the country until better times arrived. lie thought the first scheme was the best and would in the end be the cheapest. Mr. FORSHAW said he would like to point out that ' the first scheme would only cost id. in the .E. While he sympathised with the large ratepayers he thought an increase of a farthing would not be felt. They could not get behind the fact that additional accommodatioa must be provided. The Workhouse was already over- crowded. They talked about beautiful Warrington, but the real way to make Warrington beautiful was to do their duty. They were obliged to Mr. Hickman for the trouble he had gone to but as Mr. Hickman's scheme would cost £2,500 he was sure he would review the matter,especially as the money would have to be found out of the rates immediately. Mr. A. J. JOLLEY said he was surprised at the remarks made by Mr. Thompson as he thought Mr. Jenner Mist had put the matter in the clearest possible way. They could not put young girls profligate women and innocent lads with profligate men. That was ridiculous. They must classify their inmates and to classify them properly meant to increase the pressure in several de- partments. There was very great pressure indeed. Mr. NIATHER suggested that as a number of members had to leave the meeting that the matter should be ad- journed. Mr. Jor.LEY moved "That the existing accommoda- tion of the Workhouse being inadequate it is resolved that steps: be taken forthwith for the provision of proper and efficient accommodation •

Gleave notched another couple and looked like winning, when Shaw rose to the occasion. and by a succession of singles won by the odd point. Up to this period Fletcher's had had matters all their own way, and victory was assured, it being impossible for London aad North-Western to win the game. The Railway men made a game fight and the last three games going in their favour, Fletcher's final lead was reduced to one of 18 points.

The other match which was decided on Saturday was followed with great interest as in it figured last year's champions who met Itylands No. 1. team. The game opened very evenly both teams scoring 21 points in the first two games. The next stage saw the champions with a lead of six points and a game later that lead was increased to 13. Thirteen is always regareed as an unlucky number and Pearson and Knowles next had their balance reduced to eleven after a lengthy fight between J. W. Worsley and W. Povey. T. Hatton made short work of F. Miller and increased his side's advantage to one of 24.

Rylands set tkemselves to work off the balance of points against them and were led by W. Roughley who won 15-11. W. Butterworth followed with an improve- ment upon his colleagues score, and J. Shaw winning by the odd points thirteen chalks only separated the teams. T. Humphreys broke the run of success being beaten 15-11 after having been in arrear throughout his game with H. Bennett. Rylands were not however to be denied and as J. Woods triumphed over P. Ball to the extent of five points the issue of the game was still unsettled.

G. Ball and G. Shaw were the last two players on the green and a most exciting struggle they had. Ball at once took the lead but G. Shaw peeled at 13. The next two points were divided. Both were playing splendidly and in the last end Shaw got his wood home by about half an inch and claimed the game, but Peatson and Knowles won by 14 pointa.

Ball had an unusually busy time on Saturday. Early in the day he assisted the Lancashire and Cheshire team to achieve a victory over Yorkshire by defeating his opponent 21-13. Immediately after his game he came back to Warrington an assisted the Cemetery Club in their match with the Fearn- head team, winning his game 15-3. He then went on to the King and Queen Green where he was as above stated beaten by the narrowest possible margin by G. Shaw. He had hoped to do the bat trick but as it was he accomplished no small a feat, to play in three matches. Curiously enough each of the three teams for which 13al1 turned out won their respective fixtures.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE. The Cemetery players showed something like their true form on Saturday, and greatly surprised the Fearn- head men by inflicting on them a most thorough defeat. Certainly the " farmers " did not view their visit to the Cemetery Green with anything like assurance. There was the barest chance that they would win and they confidently expected they would run the ex-champion close. Their visions, however, of complete or even comparative success were rudely dispelled as the Cemetery men rose to the occasion and by winning nine out of the twelve games played triumphed in no half-hearted fashion. The improvement in the play of the Cemetery men has how- ever come a little too late in the season to be of any particular advantage as their previous poor display has relegated them to a position which it is not often their lot to occupy.

Saturday's match calls for little comment as the issue of the game was never in doubt. One of the most exciting struggles of the fixture lay between 3. Willett andfW. Bowering. The Fearnhead man went off with a rush and until the eleventh end carried everything before him the score then standing at 13-3. Willett then scored 9 while his opponent registered one but the last end going to the " farmer" he won 15-12. J E. Harrison and John Weston had a stiff struggle an after peeling 6-7 and 13 Weston won by 2 points.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

To put it mildly Warrington were surprised on Satur- day and that by no mans agreeably. Entertaining Earlestown on the Arpley enclosure they imagined victory was certain this optimistic opinion being apoirently justified by the ease with which they carried off the honours when they visited Earlestovvn but cricket is ever a game in which the unexpected happens. On Saturday E irlestown were sm irting under the reverse with which they net earlier in the season and they had their revenge, giving ‘Varrington one of the most thorough trouncings they have had this season.

It was fondly supposed—ere the season opened—that as a combination the local eleven were particularly strong but the progress of the West Lancashire League engagements has rudely dispelled that belief. Thera is no denyingthe fact that they have not by along way come up to expectations and theil anticipation that they would figure prominently and successfully in the league tourna- ment is no longer entertained by their supporters. Prior to Saturday's fixture hone did follow its usually ascribed course of springing eternally &c., but it is now recog- nized that the team has barely the remotest possibility and that a great improbability of being placed even in the first three.

Reverting to Saturday's match, Earlestown batted first and after malting a bad start proceeded to amass the respectable total of 166. On the surface Warring- ton's task did not appear a very big one and it was with light hearts that their batsmen went to face the Earles- town bowlers. Then the procession—one cannot call it by any other name—beg In and one after another the local wielders of the willow returned crestfallen to the pavilion. Podinore was the only one who could do any- thing with the bowling and his score of 22, while not a great one, stood out prominently. Only two other players; Richardson and Thompson, reached double figures.

Mason, for whose benefit tha proceeds of the match were set aside, bowled throughout the Eirlestown inn- ings, hut captured only two wickets at a cost of 53 runs. Thewlis was the most successful with the ball, and his flue wicket cost 9.75 runs each.

Sankey Wire Mills have every reason to feel proud of themselves not having been beaten so far this season. On Saturday they tried conclusions with Glazebury and won by 15 runs. During the match only two players, Taylor and Gleave of the Sankey team reached double figures, their scores being 17 :ind 18. Gleavo also figured prominently with the ball taking 7 wickets for 15 runs.

Northwich on Saturday visited Grappenliall and batting first scored only 46 runs. The apparently easy task of equalling that score was however beyond the home team who were easily defeated. The first eleven would have done well to retain the services of some of the batsmen who went with the second contingent to Northwich and there totalled 144 in reply to a total of 102.

Warrington's preservers of the peace had a close fight •vith the Co-operative team on Tuesday and were beaten by three runs. Hayes was the most successful blue taking six wickets and scoring 7 runs, the second highest score for his side.

Bowling. WARRINGTON WORKS COMPETITION.

The Works Competition is now nearing the end as to-day (Saturday) the semi-final will be decided. Four teams are left in and are Pearson and Knowles (No. 1.) Longford, Fletchers and Whitecross (No. 1.) and I am venturing upon a prophesy that last year's champions will retain that honour, in spite of the fact that a forecast which I made last week in anticipating the defeat of Fletchers in the third round by the London and North- Western men was inaccurate. instead of being beaten Fletchers won fairly easily and threaten to seriously contest the leadership.

Both teams were confident of victory but the faith of the railway men was considerably shaken %hen T. Bridge disposed of A. Peters in most summary fashion. Peters was regarded as certain to contribute to the London and North-Western success and his defeat was rather a shock to his colleagues but he had met with more than his match. .7. Nlercer made a slightly better show in that he doubled Peters' score of two, and C.

Local Athletic Successes._

At the Wallasey Charities Carnival at New Brighton Tower grounds on Saturday, J. S. Benyon, of Chester, the world's amateur kilometre champion,won the one lap bicycle handicap from scratch in 39 3-5 see., and the five miles scratch race for the Wallasey Licensed Victuallers' Challenge Cup, value 25 guineas, in 12min. 3-ssec. In the former race C. Berry, of Warrington, eame in third from the 18 yards mark, and was second to Benyon in the five miles scratch race. At the same meeting, H.:Nl:untield, of Warring- ton, won the second prize in the half-mile bicycle race from the 54 yai cis triark.C. J. Straw,of Warrington,ran in the one mile Northern Counties Athletic Association Championship, but WaS nnplaced, the victor being J. Roberts, of Sefton Harriers.

WILFUL DAMAGE. At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, Francis King, 17, Dial street, was charged with breaking a plate glass window in the Lord Rodney Inn, doing damage to the extent of ,C 2 ss. Sergeant Barnett said he saw prisoner standing close to the window and heard a smash of glass. On going up he found that prisoner had thrown a bottle through the window. Prisoner was sent to gaol for two months with hard labour.

When asked at Stratford on Wednesday if he a-ere guilty of drunkenness, James Collins made an unusual reply. "If the constable says so." he said. "I have never know a policeman to tell a lie."

WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL.

THE CORPORATION AND TRADERS' ACTION.

A Distinction Drawn Between Plaintiffs.

JUDGMENT OF THE HOUSE OF LORDS.

The House of Lords gave judgment on Monday in the appeal of Messrs, Joseph Crosfleld and Sens James Fairclough and Sons, Mark Hill and Co., and other traders and manufacturers carrying on business at War- rington and the Mayor and Corporation of Warrington the respondents being the Manchester Ship Canal Com- pany. The appeal was from an order of the Court of , Appeal reversing a decision of Mr. Justice Byrne on a , question raising a point of law—namely, whether the laigh Court had, or had not, jurisdiction to entertain the question. The action was brought principally to recover certain tolls paid to the Canal Company on the ground that certain dredging operations imposed by statute on that company had hot been fulfilled. The Lord Chancellor, in giving judgment, said :—ln this case the preliminary question of competence is alone before us. I agree entirely with what Lord Justice Cozens-Hardy has said, and I have nothing to , add to what he said. I Lord Macnaghten said that it was no wonder that the i preliminary question as to competency, which was I argued fully before their lordships, led to much differ- ence of opinion in the courts below. The provisions of the Manchester Ship Canal Acts of 1885 and 1896 as to arbitration were so various and inconsistent that it ; was impossible to arrive at a conclusion which could be regarded as satisfactory from every point of view. The question was raised for the first time when the parties went to trial. It did not seem to have been argued very seriously before Mr. Justice P.yrne, who tried the case. Mr. Justice Byrne's opinion was that the provision contained in sub-section 22 of section 88 of the Act of ;885 would have been I enough to oust the jurisdiction of the court if it had not been for the introductory words of the section, which declared that the provisions that followed shonla i have effect, "unless otherwise agreed on between the Corporation and the company." The learned Judge thought that the parties might waive a provision which they were authorised to alter by agreement. There was a good deal to be said for that view, but he was not I satisfied that mere omission to insist on a statutory I provision should not have effect. The enactment at the ;commencement of section 88 seemed to require some- thing more that tacit abandonment. Upon appeal, the decision of Mr. Justice Byrne as to competency was reversed by a majority of the mut. The action, so far as it was brought by the Corporation, was dismissed. So far as it was brought by the traders, it was stayed till further order. For some reason which he did not quite understand, Lord Justice Vaughan Williams held that the provision in sub-section 22 of section 88 in the Act of 1885 was not one of the pro- visions which the Corporation and the company were competent to deal with by agreement, and that the jurisdiction of the court as between the Corporation and the company was ousted by sub-section 22. With- out expressing a decided opinion as to whether the traders hail independent rights which they could enforce, Lord Justice Vaughan Williams came to the conclusion that such rights, if they existed, could not be enforced unless and until the default of the company had been established in an arbitration between the taorporation and the company. Lord Justice Vaughan Williams did not think it necessary to consider section 202 of the Act of 1885, which enacted that, if any question should "arise between the company and any person touching anything to be done or not to be done, or any money to be paid, under the provisions of the Act, then, unless by the Act otherwise expressly provided, such question should be "determined by arbitration in manner pro- vided by the Railways Clauses Consolidation Act, 1845." Lord Justice Stirling agreed with Lord Justice Vaughan Williams in his view of sub-section 22, and in the general result of his opinion, though he thought the traders were entitled to enforce the provisions in their favour contained in section 88, but he relied mainly, if not entirely, on section 202, which Lord Justice Vaughan Williams left out of consideration. On the other hand, Lord Justice Cozens-Hardy, while agreeing that the I LULLABY. jurisdiction of the court was ousted as between the Corporation and the Company, held that the traders had Through Sleepy-Land doth a river flow. statutory rights which they could enforce independently On its further banks wrate daisies grow; of the Corporation, and that they were not compelled And snow-white sheep, in woolly floss, to resort to arbitration or to await the result of arbitra- Must, one by one, be ferried across. tion between the Corporation and the company. I was pleased to get a little, note from Dorothy Starke. She tells me that she is stay- ing in Norfolk. lam pleased to hear from any of you, telling me of ,visits made to differ- ent parts of the country. I know Norfolk and Suffolk very well, and nave spent many days on the beautiful rivers and broads; you children living in other parts of England have no idea of these lovely places. On a bright summer day thousands of butterflies are seen flitting among the ferns. Beautiful flowers abound everywhere. On the other hand, very few places are equal to Devonshire in my estimation. You will remember that I have not long returned from my holiday. Well, I went from Lond9n straight down to Penzance, this place is nearly at the extreme end of Corn- wall, as, of course, you all know.- Some of us went across to the Scilly Isles. These are lovely islands; flowers and ferns grow almost like in a tropical country., Then we went to Ilfracombe, Exeter, Dawlish, Torquay, and back to London. . _

This week I have received a very nice letter from a member in Burton. I give this below, and I am sure that my little friends will not mind this. I will give a prize volume to the child first sending the correct answer to the paragraph, entitled "Rather Contradictory." With all good wishes from UNCLE NED.

RATHER CONTRADICTORY.

A school teacher asked her children lately if any of them could give a common English word whibh is defined as " confined or restrained," and also as "going, or ready to go," and 4` to spring, or to leap."

, Then, before they could reply, she told them that she held in her hand something that was (this word) very neatly and tastefully; "and in it,"-she added: "I notice that a boy remarked: 'I am (this word) to go swim- ming to-day.'" Whereupon, Bessie Scott, one of the scholars, said with a laugh: "And I can (this word) any hedge in Derbyshire."

Let me 'hear' from you concerning this word.
