• Burton-on-Tre: DEAR UNCLE NED,—I was never so n deliglited before, when I saw the Child; Corner appear in the "Gazette." I at

once came to the conclusion that someone cared for the children. It has, so far, been bright and chatty. I thought of you when you were away on your holidays. I hope you had a very nice time of it. We are to have our holidays on Thursday, and a month following, so we shall have plenty of time to make out the puzzles which appear in the "Gazette." I take a very great interest in the Corner, and I shall do my very best to improve it. I agree with the motto very well, and I think it is our duty to keep up the friendship which shOuld exist between us. I think it would be very nice for us to correspond with each (Abel:. I hope you will mention this to the members in the Children's Corner. I am not surprised to 'hear that your bag gets heavier week by week, for you write such interesting letters to your members. I notice that the number of members still increases, and I hope for the Corner's speedy success. lam a v•ery great lover of the game of cricket, and I took special interest in the three Test Matches already played. I daresay you will like to know a little about my pets. I have Several pigeons, chief of which is the •" Boy Bugler," My eat is called "Mary." I have nine pigeons. I should like you to write in your letter next Saturday a description of your holidays. I do so much like reading about people's outings. I can send you pieces of poetry to print in your 'paper, if you wish. The children who have already been fortunate enough to win one of these book prizes ought to be proud of them. I am sure you are very grateful in giving away so many valuable prizes. Although I have not yet conquered some of the puzzles, I hope to do sd soon if I keep at them. I think it will be very nice for you to receive ideas from the members for improving your Cotner. • I remain, yours sincerely, P. J. C. P.S.—More wishes for the Corner's success.

CYCLING GOSSIP.

BY THE CLUBMAN.

This week I occupy my column of Cycling Gossip with an account of the great reocird ride which has just taken place, and of which the whole cycling and sporting world is talking. . On the morning of Tuesday, 18th July, a small group of persons met outside the Land's End Hotel to witness the start of an attempt by Mr. G. A. 011ey, of the Anerley Bicycle Club, to beat the Land's End to John o' Groats cycle record. Among those present were Messrs. E. A. Browne, of Penzance; R. P. Conch, of Penzance; A. Price, of Birmingham; W. H. Poole, R.R.A. timekeeper, of Liverpool; Buckley. of the Anfield Bicycle Club; and a few local residents.

E STA.

was made at 8 o'clock in a slight .sbpwer, which

• soon wetted the record-breaker and his _followers

to the skin. When Penzance was reached, at 8.38, the rider was 2 minutes in front of schedule, and it was still raining; but at Ilcdrutix (27 miles) the rain had ceased, and the rider was 5 minutes in front of the schedul

At this point Mr. A. B. Rowe, of St. Day, a well. local cyclist, gave to the rider his first refreshment 'on the machine. as. Shown in our sketch abOve. The rider did not stop, -and the process of handing up the milk and food was carried out in a very, deft and workmanlike manner. Truro was passed at 10.25, and at St. Columb, which was reached at 11.36, Mr. 011ey was going strongly, and apparently in splendid condition. Two or three local cyclists had gathered at St. Kew Highway to indicate the road, and this poiht was passed at 12.35, 12 minutes inside schedule time.

It is one of the rules under which these records are competed for that no pace-makers are allowed, and those who are assisting in the ride mast never approach closer than 100 yards to him while he is mounted on the machine; therefore at different places along the road the cyclists who came out to assist passed the rider, then turned end followed, keeping their' distance, and waiting an opportunity t.to render any assistance they might be able to give. At Okehanipton the rider was just inside his schedule time, and at Exeter, which was reached at 6 o'clock, he was 1 hour in front of record, the distance up to this point being 1254 miles. Bristol 210 miles, was reached at midnight, three hours in front of record. At Bristol Mr. 011ey was massaged by a noted local masseur, of whose services the rider speaks in the highest terms. Shortly after leaving Bristol, some 13 miles from Gloucester, the rider met with his 6, ' ;hap,

i I 1 1 I

of the ride occurring, which necessitated a stoppage of some 12 or 13 minutes. At this point 01ley changed on to a new machine, which he had pre- viously ordered for the ride, although at the last moment j.ust before the start he decided to use an old machine, which had previously been ridden by him in gaining the London to Edinburgh cycle record.. The new machine immediately showed its superiority to the old one, and all the rest of the ride, a distance of 657 miles, this machine' behaved most splendidly, going through without .breakage or trouble of any kind, not even a punc- ture, in spite of extremely bad roads which were encountered later on in the North of Scotland. Now and again along the road, as the ride pro- ceed there might be seen an excited cyclist timing the progress of the ride, watch in hand, who, shouting and

would indicate how much the rider was in front of record.

At Gloucester Mr. 01ley showed a remarkable wish to go to sleep; but was persuaded' to con- tinue the ride, after a bath and rubbing with olive oil.. 011ey had designed to ride for the first 36 hours without sleep, and he was successful in doing this. The way now passed through Tewkes- bury 248 i miles,. Worcester 264 miles. Kidder- minster 274 miles, Dridgnorth 289 inils, Tarporley ri4ti miles, Wan ington 368 miles, Lancaster 420 Carnforth 426 wiles, to Kendal 442 miles, Wnich was reached at 8.30 P.M. on Wednesday, the rst sleeping-place. At 2.40 A.M. on Thursday a fresh s!art was made, after the rider had been medically examined, and reported in first-class condition to continue his journey. At this point (442 miles) he was-51 hours in front of record, and good progress was now made until Edinburgh was reached, at 5 minutes past 3, the rider being then if hours ahead of record, -10 minutes in front of his schedule, and the distance being 682 miles from the start.

While waiting for the 4 o'clock ferry boat the rider took a bath at the hotel, near Granton Ferry, after which the journey was continued through Perth to Blair • Athole, which was reached at 11.45 on Friday night, after an extremely .trying ride in the darkness on the mountain passes, with many miles of pine forests, which made the riding extremely uncomfortable and depressing. 011ey took very little food, and went almost immediately to sleep, and had to be stripped, 4ponged, and massaged as he slept. The next morning at 2.45 he was again in the saddle, riding in first-class style, for Inverness, Which was (Illy reached at 1 o'clock on Friday, 81. hours -ahead of record. After a short rest at Inverness, Kessock Ferry was crossed in a special small boat, and 011ey, who had previously sprained his ankle, found considerable relief from bathing it in the waters of the Firth as the ferry was crossed. •

Up to this point all had gone satisfactorily, but immediately upon leaving Kessock Ferry a great Lead ,wimLiindikturrukazose. which lasted hour after

flour. 'Althotigh the rider plocineu on stuthorniy, only very slow progress could be made, and the wheels sank inches deep.into the heavily -soddened roads. One of the most remarkable things in connexion with this ride was the manner in which the bicycle, which was a New Hudson, fitted with a Hub Two-Speed Gear, behaved. For the first time on a record ride ot this kind the machine gave no trouble whatever; there were no breakages, and not even a puncture after leaving Gloucester. And that this should have been so upon such extremely bad roads, and under such• conditions as were encountered in the latter part of the road, speaks volumes for the excellent workmanship and high- class material which is used ih these famous machines.

THE DREAD OF FOOD.

Weak Stomach, Lost Appetite, Bodily Weakness, Flatulence' and Despondency Cured by Mother Seigel's Syrup.

In 1595, on December 27th, Mrs. Harriet Wright, of 22, Woodville Place, Hunslet, Leeds, sent a letter to the proprietors of Mother Seigel's remedies, telling of how she had suffered for years from weak stomach, indiges- tion, loss of appetite, dread of food, wind in the sac of the stomach, great bodily weakness, and a despondent, hopeless feeling. In 1893 her sufferings became very severe, and at times she collapsed and appeared to be dying. In spite of the efforts of several doctors to aid her, shs grew intensely nervous, so weak that she could not walk, and was fed on brandy, wine, milk and other light stimulants. In one of these collapses she turned almost black, and the doctors said she had heart disease. At Ilast she used Mother Seigel's Syrup, and within a week ' felt so much benefit that the pain about her heart passed away, and she could take food without distress. Soon afterwards.she was quite well. On November 21 last, or ten years after her first letter, she again wrote to the proprietors saying :—" It is a real pleasure to me to tell you how well I have been ever since my almost miraculous cure by Mother Seigel's Syrup. My health has really been surprising, and considering how very ill I was when I commenced taking the Syrup, I think my experience proves its efficacy in cases of indigestion. "I had been under five doctors, and had taken bottle after bottle of other medicine without the least benefit, but getting rather worse all the time. I did not expect to recover, yet Mother Seigel's S3nip cured me thoroughly and permanently. I can never speak too highly of it."

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. An Antrobus Well.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were ,present Messrs. S. Woodward (presiding) C. Roylance, J. Bolshaw, H. Eyes, A. H. Talbot, T. Baxter, H. M. Davies, J. H. Davies, J.Smith,T. Wright, T. Guest, W. Harrison, G. Pilling, J. L. Jones J. Fryer, .1. Spruce, J. Whitley, C. E. Linaker, jun., Admiral Jones. Parry, W. Weir, J. B. Chadwick, J. T. Ashton (clerk) W. E. Hough (assistant clerk) J. Swinton and W.Diggle (surveyors), J. Farrington, (inspector), and Dr. Adams (medical officer).

AN ANTROBIIS WELL. A complaint was read from Mr. Gerard Eaton, of Cambridge Terrace, Hyde Park, W.,in reference to Pole- lane end pump, Antrobus. Mr. Eaton said he was in- formed by his tenants that before the Council interfered the water was bcautiful and clear like gin, but that since the Council had taken it over and cleaned (?) the well it had been nasty, thick, oily reddish stuff not tit for pigs to drink." This description seemed to Mr. Eaton to be very apt, though be might have expressed it differently. Now that the pump was open the chil- dren regarded it as a plaything,and were always troubling the water, and evidenced a lack of reverence for con- stituted authority. Since the parents demanded free education and the children free amusements, he wondered whether in the end it would not be cheaper if the Coun- cil were to provide them with a pair of parrallel bars and a swing, or the sign post by the well, and so direct their attention from the pump. In conclusion, Mr. Eaton asked the Council to restore the water to its original purity. • Mr. Farrington stated that the fault lay in the water not having yet settled since the well was cleaned out. It was gradually settling and had considerably improved when he examined it the previous day. The children would be told that they must not tamper with thr well. • The Clerk was instructed to write to Mr. Eaton stating that the matter was receiving attention. MR. SWINTON REPROVED. Mr. Woodward said his attention bad been called to an entry in Mr. Swinton's diary, Having explained his work in the morning Mr. Swinton said he spent the rest of the morning entering up this nonsensical diary. In making an expr3ssion of that description with reference to a decision of the Council he thought Mr. Swinton had exceeded his duty and had presumed upon his position and he would be remiss if he did not reprove him. Whatever the Council decided it was the duty of the °facers to guiltly acquiesce wnatever their opinion.

ANONYMOUS COMPLAINTS. Dr. Adams said he had received a number of anony- mous letters complaining of the state of a sewer at Weston Point. it was his intention to disregard any letters which were unsigned and if there was a real grievance, as there was in that instance, persons should have the courage to sign their names to the letters which they sent. Mr. Woodward : The Medical Officer is quite in the right in ignoring anonymous communications.

The Examiner, Saturday, July 29, 1905.

gradients. The sceuery i 3 to my mind more changeful and we ht.viring on the Albula how- ever.

From Tiefenkasten to 'Mush; by the Schyn Road is my favourite pass, although quite a small one. in Switzerland. It Ties in, engineer- ing skill Ivith the more renowned Via Male, but personally I consider it much its superior as regards scenery. Anyhow my readers can make the comparison if they choose, for the latter pass commences where the former ends, namely, at Thusis, and a morning can be very pleasantly spent in exploring the Via Mak as far as the second bridge, beyond which there are not such remarkable points of view. Thusis to Bonaduz is due North and good running, and from here we follow up the Rhine Vally mid are able to ride nearly the whole way to Chiamut at the foot of the Oberalp Pass. Up and over this brings us again to Andermatt Ind we continue right through this village to Realp, leaving the St. Gothard road on our left. There is a good two to three hours' walk beyond Realp over the second highest carriage road in Europe, the Furka Pass, but a grail% zig-ta descent the other side to the world re- nowned Rhone Glacier and Valley well repays the climb. Down this latter is a grand ran and this is an attractive route to the Simplon and Tete Noire passes. Zermatt, ChamPunix, the Lake of Geneva, etc., s.ud strongly recom- mended.

SOME OF THE SCENERY.

I propose, however, to keep to the Bernese Alps by scaling the newly constructed Grimsel Pass which ascends the mountain sides in long zig-zags with magnificent sweeping turns at either end.

From the summit, past the famous Falls of the Aar at llandeck to within a few miles of Meiringen (where there is a short bill to climb) La practically a " coast" for those who approve of this method of riding and it really is a mag- nificent and exhilarating ride.

Meiringen to Lucerne by the Brunig Pass is eland's play after the higher Passes but none the less picturesque for that. The surface too resembles a cinder track, especially on the Lucerne side, which town we enter frpm an opposite side to that by which we left if, after a comprehensive Alpine tour including ia all ten passes._ .

From Meiringen an alternative route to Bale would have been via Interlaken (for the Jung- frau, Lauterbrtumen Valley and 31iIrren), nun, Berne Bienne the Minister Thal and Delimont. . .

_ All, the 13ale-Calais or Bale-Paris trains stop at Delemont so there is no need whatever to ride the -few miles separating this latter place and Bale, and which owing to the traffic, are badly cut up. To give minute details as to distance, altitudes, gradients, hotels, etc., would here be out of place, the more especially that the capa- bilities and energies of every rider differ. Armed with one's Baedeker and this little resume' a very jolly, inexpensive and health giving trip of from a fortnight's to a month's duration can be arranged and I am sure that anyone who loves grand scenery and grand roads (what cyclist does not ? ) could not do better than give Switzerland a trial this year any time between June and September inclusive. The earliness or lateness of the seasons vary so much from year to year that one cannot say positively, but July and August can be relied on as the best months for. such a trip as I have outlined.

[ALL RIGHTS RESTA:VEG.] CYCLING IN THE ALPS

C. F. SIMOND (Author f " Through the Alp on a Cyck," 4c.).

A. few years ago the above heading would have caused the general run of cyclists to scoff and remark :--f` Cycling in Switzerland! Who- ever heard of such madness? One goes to Switzerland to climb mountains and scale heights inaccessible to the cyclist but to bicycle in Switzerland, why the idea is absurd !" This, as I say, was what nine persons out ot every ten used to reply when I, in answer to the enquiry as to where I had spent my holiday, would say that a bicycle tour in "la belle Suisse" had again proved so attractive to me that I had once more wheeled through the Alps much to the benefit of my general health. Bat now as every cycling season comes round, more and more frequenters of Switzerland who have hitherto only cycled at home, include their machine with other luggage" when start- ing for their yearly visit to that lovely country of snowy mountains, forests, lakes, rivers and waterfalls.

It is a well known fact that a trip to Swit- zerland creates an insatiable desire to revisit the country at the first opportunity and I think this would be proved were any of my readers to try a short tour there this year. In SwitzeK- land, as in nearly every other country, one can keep one's expenses within very reasonable limits if one has not the desire to frequent the best hotels, but Switzerland has the advantage over most other countries from the fact that it essentially land of hotels and where catering in all its classes is reduced to a veritable art. Be careful always to fix your inclusive terms be- forehand, otherwise the "extras " very often assume considerable proportions, but no doubt to the genuine touring rider, such a hint will be out of place.

===== 0002601_19050729_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
VIE QUESTION OF-LUGGAGE.

The postal arrangements throughout Switzer- land are as near perfect as possible. One can send almost any weight by post, sa that there is no need to overburden either machine or rider with unnecessary luggage. ljy despatching this in the morning before • starting the day's ride, to one's destination for the night, one will find one's luggage delivered the same day, always providing one has been careful to send aft the parcel before the morning post has gone. This can be usually ascertained from the hall porter and in case one Nis not sure of one's actual hotel for the following stop, the baggage can be sent poste restante. Then. again, telephonic and telegraphic communication all over Switzer- land is very extensive and complete, so that one can easily engage one's room for the night by telephone. I usually carry sufficient on the machine for a night's stay in case of emergencies, and have even on some occasions, where I have not been certain of the roads or accommodation, sent my " gladstone " to a destination some three or four days' ride ahead, and carried my wants on the bicycle ; but then, personally, I do not object to a little extra weight, whereas some think a very light load an absolute sine qua non for a cycling tour. Some years ago, before brakes claimed as much attention, as they very rightly do now, we used to trail a log of wood some three yards behind the machine affixed to the saddle pillar by a piece of string. This is a most effctive method of retarding the bicycle and a useful " tip " to bear in mind in case of need.

THE MACHINE.

As regards the mount itself, be sure it is in good condition, and understand it thoroughly. There are now competent repairers in almost every town of any pretensions at all in Switzer- land, but it will save considerable time and ex- pense to remedy any small defects oneself. I cannot too strongly urge a low gear, 60 at the most, and even for a tandem one of 70 is ample. By the way, this latter form of touring is strongly to be recommended for Switzerland. It is quite a fallacy to imagine that the turns are too sharp on the passes for a multicycle. Of course care is required when descending •strange roads, but then I cannot recommend Switzerland to reckless riders! A tandem is so bandy if there be any pushing ,to be done, (especially if one rider happens to be a the fair sex). Of course in a mountainous , country like Switzerland, there is bound to be & certain amount of walking, although on most of the Passes the gradient is, hardly ever of the " side of-a-house " order and can often as not be negotiated with little extra exertion. I I would recommend intending riders to study their Baedeker before starting on their tour, I for there is nearly always a gradual and a stiff ascent to a Swiss Pass and of course it is pre- ferable to leave the longer and more gradual approach for the descent. The Tipper Engadine is a case in point. Enter it from Italy by the stiff ascent to Alaloja and then you have a clear run of over 75 miles which Is a very slight decline he whole way to 3lar- tinsbriicke, the Austrian Swiss frontier. Think of 75 miles grind and a few short sharp miles' descent brakes on hard all the while as an alternative

SOME OF, TRH norTts.

And now I shall give you a few routes with the " right " order of tackling them. I assume that my readers are all members of the 0.T.0. The advantages to be gained at the various frontiers are very great. It is very advisable to have a little cycling over the plain of Switzerland to get ope in good condition for the passes. Therefore start riding directly the train deposits you in Swit- zerland; let us say for argument's sake at ale.

From this town we shall ride straight to Lucerne which makes an admirable starting point for a number of excursions, and as the St. 1 Gothard is a pass made famous by its won- derful railway and tunnel I propose to attain the Alpine fastnesses by this route. Take the steamer on, or ride round the lake ef the four Cantons to Fluelen and find your- self at the entrance to this celebrated road. One can ride a good part of the 26 miles up the valley that brings one to the northern end of the tunnel, Goeschenen, and, whence it is but a few miles to Andennatt via the celebrated Devil's Bridge. From here there is a choice of three , Alpine routes and by following my itinerary it is possible to see all in turn. Continue over the St: Gothard pass through Airolo, the scene of a great landslip, and thence down the Faido valley (On a pertect road - to Bellintona. Now make for Lugano situated on the lovely lake of that name and whence you are bound to take the steamer to Porlezza, there being no road in this direction round this pretty little lake. Before embarking, however, pass the Customs on the Quay, and give your- self plenty of time for this manceuvre. Porlezza to Menaggio consists of about five miles gradual ascent and then what cannot be avoided in this case, a somewhat stiff descent of a couple of miles. The road round the lake of Como on which Menaggio is situated, is splendid going and proves a very interesting and picturesque ride to Colico. The route now leads through Chiavenna and mounts for the most part to Casaccia at the foot of the Maloja Pass above referred to, the frontier having bees negotiated en route at Castasegne. ' Although on occasions one can get through the requisite formalities in a very little while, it is always advisable to allow oneself plenty of time when a frontier is to be included in the day's work. From Casaccia there is a stiffish ascent to lialoja and now before us is the valley of the Upper Engadine.. A good road runs along several mountain lakes to St. Moritz and by continuing straight along this valley one descends the 75 miles of splendid road above mentioned to Martinsbriicke. Here we cross the river Inn into Austria and start a climb to Nauders.

Tilt HIGHEST ROAD VI EUROPE.

--------- At this small town - commences the Stelvio route, the highest road in Europe and well worth the long climb to its smnmit. which seldom altogether free uom snow. The run down to Bormio on the Italian side is superb and unequalled anywhere in the Alps.. To re-enter the Engadine, we must leave , this valley and negotiate the Bernina Pass. This is not at all a difficult one and once the summit is reached, there is a splendid run down to Pontresina with the wind almost invariably at one's back. The beauties of the Allier and Albula Passes render it difficult to decide which to take, but I think on the whole the latter is more to be recommended. Both ultimately lead to Tiefenk- asten and are ecnial iu reaucct of mad,.

THE LIBERAL CAMPAIGN. ENTHUSIASTIC MEETING IN ST. JOHN'S WARD.

The conduct of the Liberal campaign in Warrington is being energetically continued and on Wednesday night an enthusiastic meeting was held in St. John's Ward. It had been arranged to hold the gathering in the open air, but on account of the rain which fell heavily an adjournment was made to the Sharp-street mission, which despite the inclemency of. the weather was !crowded, the large attendance being eloquent testimony to the growing popularity of the Liberal cause. 'Mr. G-. Walker presided and was supported by Councillor Maginn, Mr. Neilson, Liberal and Labour candidate for the Newport Division of Shropshire, Mr. J. Howes and others. The CHAIRMAN said that as a convinced Free Trader he had no sympathy with the nostrums of Mr. Joseph Chamberlain. If Mr. Chamberlain was as anxious as he would have us believe he was to improve our conditions, he should turn his attention to the taxation of ground rents and royalties, which rested so heavily upon our manufacturers and industries. (Applause). Councillor NTAG INN, who was accorded a hearty recep- tion, said that a week ago many of them thought they were to have a general election, but their experience of Mr. Balfour should have taught them that there was no honourable standard by which they could measure the length of time he would hold on to the reins of office and mismanage the country. Mr. Balfour would have to give the country an account of his stewardship and when the time came for him to do so they knew perfectly well what the verdict would be, and the punishment they would inflict upon the party which had done what the Tory party had done and reduced the country to the state which it was in at present. (Applause.) The Tory Government with its powerful majority had for the past five years wasted the time of Parliament and increased the number of unemployed in the country. Never within the know- ledge of any man was there a time in the history of the country when there were so many men starving as there were at the present time. That was perfectly well- known, and yet we were told by the Government that an Unemployed Bill could not be introduced this year. The solution of that great problem lay in

LAND REFORM. —(applause)—and that must be the first plank in the Liberal platform if they intended to improve the wretched state of the country. (Applause.) They were bound to insist that the workingpeople of this country should not be driven from the land to the towns. The state of Ireland had a great deal to do with the state of employment in England.By the land laws in that country —lawswhich were passed for the benefit of the land- lords and the grazers—the people were forced to emi,,brate and instead of living comfortably at home they came to England and entered into competition with the English workman. After all what was Home Rule? It was simply that the Irish should be permitted to manage their local affairs as every English borough did, and surely no one could manage local affairs better than the people on the spot. Ireland was a land of ruined homes and farms and instead of main- taining a population of 20,000,000 people as it is per- fectly capable of doing if 01 the available land was placed under cultivation, the population has been reduced to four and a half million souls. With better land laws Ireland could be prosperous and contribute to the taxation of England instead of being, as it was at the present time, a drain upon the imperial exchequer. The welfare of the country was in the hands of the people, and if they only thought for themselves they would never again be LEGGED DOWN BY FALSE PROMISES as they were five years ago by the gentleman who had mismangsd the affairs of the country and managed the affairs of the brewers and the landlords. (Applause.) Mr. NEILSON said it was strange how during the present crisis the government was re-awakening its old cries. They were getting out their old, worn, tattered banners and would ask the people of the country to follow their drum, and give them their votes, and enable them to continue the present state of affairs under the impression that after IS out of the past 20 years of Tory government the people loved to be gulled. The country had not been in such a terrible state as it was at the present time since the repeal of the corn laws. It was not the duty of the Government in the literal sense to feed the starving people of this country, but it was their duty to legislate in such a manner that those people who were hungry were only brought to that state of destitution by an extraordinary combination of events. (Applause). How had the present state of unemployment been brought about ? Take the increase in expenditure. During the past ten years it had gone up from £95,000,000 to £142,000,000. The expenditure on the army had doubled during the period and yet Lord Roberts described the state of the army as being di.,gmceful,and Mr. Arnold Foster said "The condition of the army was a menace to the empire." It was A SCANDAL AND A CRIMINALITY for the Tory Government to continue to increase the amount of money which was spent on a thing, which, in the words of gentlemen able to judge, was worthless to us. (Applause.) We had adopted a two standard power for our navy and spent more money on it than three continental nations put together, but had we got a navy? Our navy used to be a proud tradition but he realized that at the present time all was not well with the navy. The state of things became such that the finest naval expert we possessed, Admiral Sir John Fisher, was called in and he immediately condemned over 100 ships as being obsolete. Most of those vessels had been built during the past 10 years, and many were actually condemned before they were built, and yet that occurred during the administration of a party who said You must not trust these Liberals with your Army and Navy." The enormous amount of money which the Army and Navy took out of our pockets was

THF: CHIEF CAUSE OF BAD TRADE in the country. During the past ten years, according to the Board of Trade estimate, the purchasing power of the sovereign had been reduced by 2s. 6d. It was an economic and indisputable fact that that was another great cause of the unemployment in the country. It followed as night followed the day that when rates were high wages were low, and wages were scarce. The foreigner did not make work scarce in England. It was the present Government who, if they bad the pluck of a mouse would at once pro- claim their approval of Mr. Chamberlain's proposals. Mr. Chamberlain would remedy the present state of things by Tariff Reform, but he defied any Tariff Reformer to point to a single instance where Protec- tion had raised the wages in the country. (Applause.) Protection in England would push the 12,000,000 people who were on the verge of poverty below the line and bring another 12,000,000 on to the line. (Applause.) It was time the people thought for themselves ; England was never in such a state. The country stood in great jeopardy. It made his heart bleed when he thought of the canting millionaires going to church on Sundays, singing and praying and of the millions in the land who were ill-clad and almost starving. What a farce our latter-day Christianity must be to allow such things. If the people only recognized their power they would swamp Toryism and vandalism for ever. "Men," continued the speaker call your souls your own. You have your brains think these matters out for yourselves and do your duty as citizens by thinking for yourselves." We had a retrogressive Government in power,continued Mr. Neilson, which was trying to take our privileges from us. They had given us a most unconstitutional Education Act which denied representation with taxa- tion, and no one could say that the children were being any better educated than they were previously.He would be glad when the time came when people would say "We don't need men to teach us about politics we wilt think and act for ourselves." They must do so at the next election because it presented the greatest oppor- tunity which the wolkingclasses had ever had. At the next election they must return a Liberal Government and see that they turned their attention to

MEETING AT BRIDGE FOOT.

It is some time since there was a crowd at an open- air political meeting so large as that'Which assembled at Bridge Foot on Thursday evening to hear addresses in support of the Liberal cause. While the gathering was large there was a complete absence of any display of antagonistic feeling, and from time to time the utter- ances of the gentlemen on the platform were greeted by outbursts of applause. Mr. E. Osborne presided, and was supported by Mr. J. Howes,Mr. Neilson, Councillor Maginn, Mr. Elwell, and Mr. J. A. J. Morton. The CIIA.IRMAN alluded briefly to the way in which the Prime Minister had declined to study the people of the country. The working people of the country had gone to Mr. Balfour and asked for certain measures and he had refused them bluntly and sarcastically, and declined to consider those matters as they ought to be considered. So far as the Labour League was concerned, they were determined that in Warrington the victory should be on their side. (Applause.) Mr. NEILSON delivered a lengthy and vigorous address. Toryism, he said, had always in England been detrimental to the interest of those who formed the bone and substance of the nation—the working classes. If labour was to come to its own it depended entirely upon the way in which labour voted, and it was the desire and one of the chief aims of the earnest Liberal that there should be a system of registration and electoral reform, so that we should have in the House of Commons men who were very different from those we bad to-day. (Applause.) Mr. Neilson proceeded to refer to the question of

===== 0002601_19050729_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
ALIEN IMMIGRATION.

In 1895, he said, the Tory party went to the country with a programme, one of the chief things in which was the question of alien immigration. We were told that the question was a serious one but like many other serious questions which the Tory party faced they forgot about it and nothing was done for nine years. Last year it was feared that a general election would be hurried upon the country and wanting an electioneering cry the Tory Government once again raised the question It was said by the Board of Trade that between 60,000 and 70,000 aliens came every year to this country but it was not pointed out that only one tenth of that number remained here, and that England to the remainder was merely a halting-place to those fleeing from protec- tionist countries. Last session the Government brought in an Aliens Bill but it was not passed owing the Tories said to the obstruction of Mr. Winston Churchill. lithe Government had wanted to pass the Bill they would have done so. They passed the Education Act and Licensing Act in face of the strenuous opposition of the Liberal party and they could have done the same with the Aliens Bill. They however, did not want to pass it. It was nothing more or less than • "

AN ELECTIONEERING CRY and at the best was unworkable. The Government had itself admitted that it was unworkable by introducing a different Bill this session. (Applause.) In the United States they had the most vigorous laws to keep out undesirable aliens and yet only one out of every 100 immigrants was sent back. Let us take the same rate for England and striking an average of the number of aliens who remained here at 8,000 we would see that with laws as vigorous as they were in the United States we should keep out 80 men, women and children ! He was as anxious as anyone to keep out the diseased and criminal alien and a party of Liberals and Tories conferred to bring in a measure with that for its object but Mr. Balfour would not have it.lt was not the poor wretches who came here as steerage passengers fleeing from protection and conscription that we wanted to keep out but the men who came saloon and who when they got here took the work out of English hands and even sweated the British workingman. (Applause). If the Conservative Government wanted to show their desire to benefit workingman was sincere let them pass a bill to prevent sweating by anyone whether alien or Britisher. (Applause). Turning to the question of protection. Some people objected to the posters which were placed on the walls depicting a big and little loaf but he could speak from experience that the poster was no exaggeration.ln Germany a four pound white loaf cost four times as much as it,did in England and New York it cost' three times as much. Protection it was stated was going to raise our wages. If that was so how was it that men working for a firm which paid 15 per cent. dividend earned no more that men working for a firm which paid 5 per cent ? Let him show what protection did in the United States. Under the Carnegi regeme the workpeople were happy but during the two years of the existence of the steel trust wages had been reduced by 16,000,000 dollars while a food combine had raised the cost of living 63 per cent. _TIER STERL TRUST _ _ _

admitted they could make steel rails atl2 dollars a ton. The Canadian and Pacific Railway, which were in the position of foreigners to the United States, required several thousand tons of steel rails and were quoted by the Steel Trust twenty dollars a ton. American rail- ways were quoted by the some people 28 dollars a ton, the difference in the two quotations being the exact amount of the duty upon steel rails imported into the United States. (Applause.) Those who backed Mr. Chamberlain's Tariff Reform proposals would have the same state of things exist in England. The Liberal fight was a crusade against monopoly, and it was for the working people to gain the victory. What was the good of taking about Empire when we had hungry children going to school ? The working people must study the question for themselves. He was sure they would not despair. It might be long before we had the Parliament which would be the true democratic Parlia- ment but we would go on until we achieved that end. (Applause). Councillor MAGINN said we boasted we were the richest country in the world but what was the use of asking a poor hungry man to look imperial and to think imperially when he was starving imperially. (Applause.) I The Prime Minister had been asked what it was I intended should be done with regard to dealing with ' the unemployed question and he replied that the ques- tion had been discussed for generations but that nothing could be done as the Government had to pass the Scotch Church Bill. What a "clout" for the electors who put him in power to be told that nothing could be done with the unionployed because of the " wee frees" and the "muckle frees." The present Government had I PROMISED MANY TIIINGS. They had promised free education. but they gave an Education Act instead. They promised a free breakfast table and put a 4d. tax on tea and doubled the price of sugar. They promised South Africa for the British. How many Britishers were there who had found a home in South Africa? (Applause.) He was sorry there were some workingmen who would still vote for a Government which had given us Chinese slavery but he was glad to think that they were in a minority. The future of the country was in the hands of the working- men and they must not complain if everything was not as they desired it should be. (Applause.)

MT. HOWES described the supporters of the present Government as the enemies of the country. He believed they would emancipate Warrington at the next general election. (Applause.) Mr. R. ORRELL : Will you allow me to say a few words?

The CHAIRMAN: We will answer questions but can- I not have speeches. Mr. R. ORRELL : I say this— The CHAIRMAN: I want you to understand I can- not have any introductions to questions. If you ask a fair question we will give a fair adswer. Mr. ORRELL : Mr. Neilson said the Conservative prrty were opposed to the interests of the labouring classes of the country—(A Voice : "So they are ") but is it not a fact that the Conservative party placed on the Statute Book the repeal of the Combination laws ? The Tories stormed against measures which the Liberals sent up but they passed them years later, after they had cut their wisdom teeth, and even then would not be able to pass them if it were not for the Liberal vote. He would guaranteed that investigations would show that in the majority of cases things passed by Tory government had been introduced by the Liberals. Were they going to allow the Tories any longer to im- pose upon the credulity of the country with their elec- tioneering cries. The Workmen's Compensation Act was the mangled remains of an act introduced by Mr. Asquith long before the Tory government took it up, 'lt had been mangled but if the Liberals were returned to power they would see it straightened and would see that the workman got his compensation without the psesent legal difficulties. Mr. HowEs continuing, said that when the present Employers Liability Bill was presented for a second reading the Tory member for Belfast moved an amend- ment which if carried would, as Mr. Chamberlain pointed out mean the wrecking of the Bill. The Labour and Liberal members had a conference and decided that the Bill must be saved and when the division bell rang and the Tories in the House of Commons refused to support their own Bill, they marched into the Tory lobby and the Bill was saved. It was afterwards discovered that if the Liberals had remained in their seats the Bill ! would have been lost by 22 votes. (Applause.) He I presumed the Tory Government would claim to have passed that measure. The Examiner, Saturday, July 29, 1905.

PARR'S BANK LIMITED.

Mr. Cecil F. Parr and Trade.

A WARNING AGAINST OVER-PRODUCTION.

An extraordinary general meeting of Parr's Bank Limited, was held at the bank premises, Winwick- street, on Thursday morning. Mr. Cecil F. Parr pre- sided, there were present, Sir David Gamble Messrs. T. H. Dixon, John Dunn, Alfred Hewlett, Robert Neill, 1 and Peter Stubs (directors), Messrs. R. W. Whalley (general manager), J. Addison,JohnAinsworth, C. Baker, W. H. Boothroyci, E. C. Cooper, David Garnett, W. Gill, Joseph Hartley, A. Jolley, A. Lister-Kaye, J. Lawson, W. Moss, W. Owen, J. C. Steed, J. Wainwright, C. White, W. Skinner, J. L. McGregor, W. Silcock, and E. D. Hyatt (secretary) The Cu AIRMAN said they once more had met together to render an account of their stewardship and he thought they would agree with him from the study of the report that it was of a satisfactory kind. They had no difficulty in lending their money this half year, but it had not been at very satisfactory rates. The Bank of England rate had ruled very low, it had been £2 13s. 9d. which was no less than 18s. lower than it was the corresponding period of last year. Fortunately for themselves, they had had some Japanese money to deal with at short periods and the employment of that had helped them in the return of their profits. They had to regret the resignation of Mr. Crewdson, one of their Manchester directors. He found himself unable any longer to give his services to the bank at Manchester. He was very glad to say that the bank possessed two very able directors in the person of Sir J. J. Harwood, who was unable to be present that day, and Mr. Neill, and therefore they did not at present contemplate filling the vacancy caused by Mr. Crewdson's retirement. As a review of the half year, it could not be said that trade generally was showing any marked improvement at present. It was certainly true that the total figures in the aggregate value of the exports and imports again showed a record, more especially in the case of exports, which were a good ten millions beyond the figures for the corresponding six months of last year. This he thought was chiefly due to the general rise in prices of commodities, and therefore it was no proof of any considerable expansion in volume of transactions and trade generally. Certainly if they turned to the contemplation of revenue returns they would find reason to believe that prosperity was rather going back than coming on, for those figures showed a full million less than they did at this time last year. There was one trade however, which he was very glad to say they were able to congratulate upon a very !prosperous half year. He need not say that he alluded to the cotton trade, and equally satisfactory results should appear during the next six months pro- vided that no trouble should arise between masters and men. He thought the spasmodic attempts they had lately seen 'in America to raise unduly the price of raw material could hardly meet with much continued success in face of the present situation which obtained. The latest advices put the present crop—the new crop— at over 11,000,000 bales, which was above the average, and in addition to this, there was what bankers would call a " carry forward " of over 2,000,000 bales from the crop of last year after satisfying all require- ments. Moreover,spinners for the present must to some extent have rendered themselves independent of tem- porary fluctuations caused by those market speculations, because most of them should have brought forward laigely in the early months of this year, and so have no need of going into the market for the present. But though profitable business for same time ahead would seem to be assured from the mills already in existence, and the machinery already put up, it was a different matter, and he could not help doubting, whether the present favourable conditions showed sufficient appear- ance of permanency to justify the erection of the numerous new spinning mills, which he understood was taking place over a considerable part of Lancashire. (Hear, hear.) It was quite possible for another year or even more that there might be a sufficiency of raw material at moderate prices for the employment of those new spindles, but it seemed to him that the question to , be asked was, was Lancashire sure of an extended market for that increased output when it appeared ? They must remember the competition of other countries was daily becoming more and more severe. Japan, while at war of course, had during the year been one of their best customers, but as soon as peace was concluded, she would become to judge from the energy they had seen displayed in every other province, instead of a cdstomer, one of their most energetic competitors in the Eastern market. Besides, both in Germany and the U.S.A., in respect to their home and foreign trade, they were increasing far more rapidly in proportion than British. The country was also exporting in ever- increasing auantities machinery to those countries which would in time do their own spinning and weaving. That of course, of necessity meant not only would they in future take less of this country's productions but they would compete all themore keenly as years went by in the neutral markets. Therefore it seemed to him quite doubtful whether, if they produced much more they would be able to sell what they produced. Coming for a moment to the kindred trade of wool, certainly wool growers had for some time past been getting most excellent prices for their produce, and the demand at present seemed in no wise likely to slacken especially in America where they were asking for more and more. As long as high prices prevailed, they must expect spinners and manufacturers in this country to curtail rather then add to their commitments. To come now to the iron trade one would think there could be no possible demand to justify the enormous production which was now going on in the world of pig iron. In America alone, the pro- duction of pig iron had doubled itself almost in less than seven years, and the estimate for the present year was no less than 22,000,000 for America alone. The ' increase too, in Germany was steadily rising though not

THE MANAGER AND STAFF. Mr. JOHN Dun: proposed a vote of thanks to the general manager, the assistant manager and the staff for their services during the past year. (Hear, hear.) In establish- ing that great reputation in the City of London, he had done great servicasto the bank. One boy (H. Dutton) obtained the 17th place The CHAIRMAN expressed the gratification of the in the first class among the senior candidates. directors for the vote just passed. (Applause.) Of then ighteeu successful candidates six

pas-sed in mathematics,-french, arithmetic and scripture knowledge,—all but one passed in Latin and Greek, and all but two in history. Seven junior candidates were offered for highest mathematics and all seven were successful. A very important step forward—and one which clears the way for a general "leaving" certificate —has been made this year. It is this--that a senior certificate exempts the holder from the Matriculation Examination of the University of London andalso from the "Little-go" or "Previous Exam." at Cambridge provided that it satisfies conditions. Three of the candidates have satisfied all the conditions. The result of the Oxford locals for this year are of course not yet to hand—but it will no doubt interest you to hear what the school Examiner has to say about us. He writes: "I am again able to report favourably of the examination I have conducted at Warrington Grammar School. Indeed the impressions I have previously formed of the thorough soundness and genuine character of the teach- ing given at the school have been decidedly deepened. The school is well equipped and staffed, and the new laboratory and lecture room will prove valuable acquisi- tions. The discipline and tone of the school, so far as they came under my observation, are excellent. The curriculum is very wide, and this makes me the more gratified at the success achieved. Among the younger boys are some very promising lads, and the work in all the Forms is uncommonly even. The Junior school shows evidence of careful training in elementary work, and the results are quite in keeping with the high character of the work in the Upper Forms." (Applause.) The past year has been one of much anxiety. The school has been practically re-organized, and is now recognized by the Board of Education in London as an efficient secondary school, such recognition enabling us to earn the Government grant. This step has necessitated many changes and much-needed improve- ments. A new lecture room and a physics laboratory have been added to the existing accommodation, the teaching staff has been strengthened, and the curriculum has been modified. There is now a fully developed modern side. French is taught orally to the little boys before they begin Latin. German and Greek are now alternative subjects. More time is given to science and to modern languages and history and English subjects have received special attention during the year and in addi- tion all the more modern methods of teaching mathe- matics have been introduced. (Applause). I am glad of this opportunity to thank the Assistant Masters for the zealous and cordial way in which they have at all times co-operated with me, and also I must not forget the boys who have shown throughout the year the true spirit of work. The successes and appointments of past and present boys during the year include :—L. G. Corney, head of the school, who has won in open competition an exhibi- tion of £3O per annum for classics at St. John's College Cambridge. H. Dutton, passed the London University Matriculation Examination in the First Division. 0. H. Cooke, of Merton College, Oxford, has graduated 8.A., in the Final Honour School of Jurisprudence. C. W. Reynolds, of St. John's College, Cambridge, has graduated B.A. First Class in his special subject Chemistry. T. H. Oliff, has completed his course at the;Camborne Mining'School and in the final examination has secured 7 First Class passes and has been awarded a first class certificate in mining engineering. S. B. Han- kinson,the final Examination of thegnstitute of Bankers. H. L. Knapman, the preliminary examination of the Royal Institute of British Architects, A. D. McGregor, the Civil Service Law Examination of the University of Cape Town' K. W. D. Macrae, the Intermediate Examination for the degree of M. 8., Edinburgh University. H. Gibson, the Final Examination of the Institute of Chartered Accountants, J. W. Box and W. Green, of St. Mark's College, Chelsea, the Intermediate Examination for the degree of 8.A.. London University, also recommended by the Board of Education to receive their parchments as Certificated Teachers without taking the Special Examination. We have also to congratulate Mr. E. W. Coates, M.A., late scholar of St. Catharine's College, Cambridge, on his appointment by the Egyptian Government to an important Mastership in the Secondary School at Cairo. Also Mr. E. Frank Wilicocks, who has been appointed by the attorney- General of Natal, Clerk of the Peace and Public Prosecutor for the Inanda Division of the Colony. (Applause.) The Rev. CA.NON CUNDEY, then, amidst applause, pre- sented the prizes. Addressing the gathering he spoke of the pleasure which it gave him to be present, especially as he had some pleasing rerninescencies of his connection with the Grammar school, Ile was there formerly in the capacity of Examiner and could speak from experience of the excellent character of the work at that school. (Applause). Since then the whole organization of education had been altered and the Education Act of 1002 had had the effect of arous- ing the country to the importance more particularly of Secondary Education. It was now possible for public authorities to stimulate the work of Secondary schools in a manner which those who were teaching 20 years hardly ventured to dream of, and whatsoever might be said of the Act he thought the result so far had been in the right direction. (Hear, hear.) He was glad their local education authority had viewed with a favourable eye. what was going on in those schools and if there were any members of the Education Committee present he ventured to ask them to trust Mr. Wilcocks and his staff to give the best possible educa- tion which could be given in a school of the character of the Grammar school, as an Examiner he had always been able to report that the school was up-to-date, and he had no doubt that if the opportunity was given Mr. Willcocks would keep the school in the forefront among the best schools in the country. (Applause.) At the present time the educa- tional aspect was in danger of being too utilitarian and when it was proposed to teach a subject people were prone to ask "what is the good of it so far as the boy's future life is concerned ?" That is not the thing to have in view in drawing up the curriculum of a school. The first requisite for a Secondary school was to give a good general education and to send forth to

the world a cultured lad capable of holding his own in any walk in life. (Applause.) Having got that founda- tion, and he knew they had it there, they could safely leave specialization to later days and to the subsequent educational career of the child. The headmaster in a school of that description should be given a con- siderable amount of freedom. His experience of education authorities, and he had had many years I experence was that they were prone to interfere with the detail of a school instead of appointing a head master and giving him a perfectly free hand. He had not had the slightest hint that there was anything of that sort in Warrington. On the contrary, Mr. Willcocks was receiving every possible support from the Education Authority. The danger in a purely technical school was that the boy's general education was neglected, and he became specialized before his time in some branch of knowledge which had very little connection with humanity, but rather connected more with the sordid conditions of life. The idea of the promoters of the Education Act was to retain those schools which were wanted, and which were successful, and to stamp out of existence those schools which did not deserve to live. He hoped Mr. Willcocks and his staff would continue to receive support from those who had the privilege—and he was sure it was a privilege—to conciliate something towards the better equipment of the schools, as ungrudg- ingly as it had been given in the past. (Applause.) Mr. Cnndey concluded with an appeal to parents to allow their children to remain at school as long as they possibly could. (Applause.) The students during the afternoon were afforded an opportunity of displaying their histrioric abilities, plays in French, Greek and English being given. Throughout they acted well and afforded excellent entertainment for the visitors. The plays and the dramatis personal were: " Appartment Meubl4 Loner."—Alphonse Duval (jeweller), R. S. McMurray ; Edgar Colombe (draper), H. 0. Adams ; Bond (American Traveller), J. Whitehouse: Madame Bonivard, R. Wilcox and Rose (her daughter), W. M. Watkins. THE PROPOSED NEW SCHOOLS AT STOCKTON HEATH. Opposition to the Scheme.

A special meeting of the Stockton Heath Parish Council was held in the Hall Cafe, VintoHa-square, on Wednesday evening, to receive the report of the joint committees of the school managers and representatives of the Stockton Heath, Walton Inferior, and Latchford Without Councils. Walton Inferior having already,it is understood, lodged an objection to the County Council proposal on the ground that the school is too large,were not present at the committee meeting. On Wednesday night when the following resolution was submitted. "This meeting is of opinion that in considering new school requirements Stockton Heath should be treated separately from the adjoining parishes, which should provide for their Own requirements, and that if that were done Stockton Heath would need no accommoda- tion beyond the present schools." Its adoption was moved by Mr. J. W. Bouchier who pointed out that if children in attendance at the present schools who came from Warrington, Latchford and Walton were excluded the schools would be ample. Mr. Hallows seconded, objecting to the proposed new school on account of the increase which its erection would make in the rates. . .

Several other members spoke in support of the reso- lution which was unanimously approved. Latchford Without will lodge a separate objection to the scheme. PUBLIC PETITION TO TFIE BOARD OF EDUCATION. In Stockton Heath a petition is being drawn up, copies of which the ratepayers will be able to sign in I almost every shop in the village. The petition, which 1 is addressed to the Board of Education, reads : (1). That the Cheshire County Council as the local Educa- tion Authority have given statutory notice of their intention to provide a new public elementary school Ito accommodate about 800 scholars, in the parish of 1 Stockton Heath, such school to be available for 1 the parishes of Stockton Heath, Latchford Without, and Walton Inferior. (2). That tho present School accommodation in the parish hc f elementary Stockton Heath is amply sufficient to provide for the requirements of that parish considered by itself and that the proposed new school is not required in the said parish. (3). That the overcrowding at the exist- lag school is due to the presence of scholars from outside ; parishes and your Petitioners respectfully urge that the burden to provide accommodation ought not to be orne even in part by the parish of Stockton Heath, but that those parishes which occasion the necessity for further school accommodation ought to make provision for it and bear the expense. (1). That your petitioners are prepared to go into the figures and state their case I in opposition to the proposed new school and they accordingly suggest with great respect and pray that a j local inquiry should be held in order that your petitioners may be heard.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

The Want of Swimming Instruction. Sir,—Recently two ladies called at the Warrington Baths. They wished to take lessons in swimming. The manager told thorn he was not permitted to teach swimming. They were surprised. And when they understood that no one—lady or gentleman—was pro- fessionally engaged in teaching swimming at the Baths they were astounded. That was their mental state when they left. They would be indignant later. And eventually they would laugh—perhaps they are laughing i yet—for the situation s almost Gilbertian. The manager may save from drowning, but not—if you please—instruct a man how he may save himself. Public baths are erected just BB much to encourage the natatory art as habits of cleanliness, the plunge baths being for swimming, not washing, purposes. The non- swimmer's fear of cold water—clear or dirty—is very natural. For water to him is a tickle and terrible element. Learning to swim without instruction is very often a slow process hut a few lessons from a capable teacher make a wonderful difference in the progress of the most inapt pupil. Surely something might be done. There are scores of parents in this town who desire that their children should be taught to swim. I know that here—as in other towns—it seldom pays a pro- fessional swimmer to attend at the baths to give lessons, and obviously Mr. Barlow, who is on the spot, could conveniently do 80, selecting a quiet hour for the purpose. In other towns it is expected that a manager will teach swimming—or the committee would wonder why he applied for such a position. Of what advantage is it to have a manager a man of Mr. Barlow's qualifications as:a swimmer and teacher of swimming ? People in difficulties can be fished out of the water very quickly with the long poles provided—in quicker time than a ;swimmer could reach them, perhaps. Maybe some members of the Baths Committee think teaching would interfere with Mr. Blrlow's other dutied, but surely it is only a matter of arrangement.—Yours ..tc„ GiODFBEY DEAN. Bewsey Street, Warrington.

DEATH OF MR. D. DAVIES. A Prominent Cyclist.

We record with deep regret the death of Mr. David Davies, which occurred at the Warrington Infirmary early on Wednesday morning. Until a short time ago Mr. Davies, who was in his 03rd year, enjoyed the best of health. He was however, taken seriously ill a few weeks ago, and Dr. Kisby was at once called in. More alarming symptoms developing, Dr. Langdale was also called in and it was found necessary to remove him to the Infirmary. Here, on Sunday evening,he was successfully operated upon for a congestion of the bowels but he gradually grew weaker and died as above stated. Mr. Davies was principally, known as a most enthusiastic cyclist and amongst local wheelers it would be difficult to find a more widely known and highly respected gentleman. As an active member of the Warrington Cycling Club for the past 15 years he has held the offices of president and vice-president and so popular was he that he was familiarly known to his fellow members as "Pa." As recently as July Bth he took part in the club's annual run to Beeston Towers, and a few weeks previously he, in company with Mr. Joseph Podmore, indulged in a 300 mile tour in the Midlands. Although not a public man in the generally accepted sense he was closely associated with local bodies of a charitable nature. In 1902 he did an invalu- able work in assisting in the organization of the Fancy Dress Cycle Parade and Carnival as the result of which about .£3OO was raised for the Infirmary and District Nursing Association. The parade on behalf of the Life- boat institution also had his whole-hearted and active support and latterly he devoted a large amount of time to the work of the Workingmen's Committee, of which Ihe was chairman, to raise funds in aid of the memorial to thome members of the South Lancashire Regiment who fell in the war in South Africa, and had he lived it would have been the event of his life to have witnessed ' the formal unveiling of that memoriAl. A Warringtonian, he was the son of Mr. Davies who at one time kept a tin and locksmith shop in Lyme-street. He, early in life, entered the service of a railway com- pany, and was for some years stationmaster at Church, where, during the time of the Fenian outrages, he was called upon to assist in defending an expected attack on the railway. Leaving the service of the company he obtained an excellent appointment at Sheffield, but I ultimately returned to Warrington, where up to the , time of his death, he acted as chief clerk for Messrs. Thewlis and Griffiths, file and tool manufacturers, , Mersey-street. by whom he was held in high esteem. • Of a genial disposition, he made many friencis and few, if any, enemies, and his death will be very ividely and deeply regretted. The funeral will take place at the Warrington Cemetery this (Saturday) afternoon at 3 o'clock.

AWFUL RAILWAY DISASTER

SOUTHPORT ELECRTIC EXPRESS WRECKED.

Twenty-three Passengers Killed.

A terrible railway disaster occurred on Thursday evening on the Liverpool to Southport line of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway to the electric express train which left the Exchange Station Liver- pool, at ten minutes past six o'clock full of passengers. Near Hall-road station,eleven miles from Liverpool, the express collided with an empty stationary train which. was being shunted on to a loop line. There was an appalling crash, followed immediately by awful scenes- The first coach of the express was a third-class smoker. It is stated that by the force of the collision the body of this carriage was thrown completely off the steel framework and then fell back upon the passengers, killing nearly all those whom the first shock had not deprived of life. There were 27 passengers in the coach. Twenty-three were killed, three injured, and only one escaped. The injured lay imprisoned with the dead among the wreck- age—a dreadful sight, the horror of which was intensified by the fact that a number of the killed were children. The work of succour,was commenced at once, and the - three injured passengers from the first coach were taken, to the Bootle Infirmary. They are : Henry Albert Potter, 59, Poulton-road, Southport. Albert Potter, his son. The motorman, named Rimmer. 4. - LIST OF THE KILLED. Meanwhile the dead bodies had been removed to the station waiting-rooms to await identification. Here many pathetic scenes were witnessed as relatives identi- fied their dead. At a late hour at night several remained unidentified. The following is a list of the victims : A. J. Vickery (male), Bold-street, Liverpool. C. H. Hawkes, Aughton-road Birkdale. Sydney Ellis, No. 46, Talbot-street, Southport. John R. Inch, Birkdale. Ernest Lewis, Liverpool-road, Birkdale. Thomas Howard, a youth, Formby. Stanley T. Waugh, No. 71, Avondale-road, Southport, son of Rev. Thomas Waugh. Miss N. L. Mussham, Regent-road, Birkdale. Benjamin Sykes. Freshfield-road,Formby, flour miller, and provender dealer, with mills in Brownlow Hill, Liverpool, and many shops throughout the city. James Sykes, brother of the above, 109, BrownloW Hill, Liverpool. Miss Jane Wright, 17 years, Halsall-lane, Formby. Annie Wright, six years of age, Halsall-lane, FormbY, niece of the above, Annie S. Newell, Formby. Mrs.Vtloir, Balfour-street, Southport. May Stringer, a little girl,No. 22, Sykes-street, Liver- pool. Richard Peet, Crossens, Southport. Thomas Cullen, Bisphara-road, Southport. Jennie Curphey, Formby. Esther A. Parr, Hall-street, Southport. The bulk of the killed belong to Southport and Formby, in which places the news of the disaster created consternation. Crowds flocked to the stations, many making their way down the line, as soon as anY traffic was restored, in search of missing relatives. A number of the uninjured passengers in the express, together with the station officials and others, assisted in smashing the woodwork to get at those embedded in the wreck, only a few of whom showed any signs of life. Medical aid and police help was despatched as quicklY as possible from the Liverpool side, and breakdown gangs from both termini,but it was half past nine before the one`from Southport could arrive owing to the dis- organised service, the line from Liverpool being blocked for several hours. By this time many thousands hnu collected on the golf links by the scene of the disaster, where the removed debris had been converted into huge bonfire.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

Lancashire County Union.

A meeting of the above body was held at rbc Grosvenor Hotel, Manchester, on Tuesday, Mr. F. W. Wright presiding. The Runcorn Club was elected to membership. Mr. W. H. Cawley (Runcorn) was appointed a member of the committee. The draw for the first round of the Lancashire Senior Cup was made as follows :—Rochdale Hornets Y. War- rington, Chadderton v.
